- Important terms -
Term Definition
Abscission In plants, the falling of leaves, flowers, fruits, or stems at the end of a growing season due to the formation of a two-layered zone of specialized cells (abscission zone), often triggered by the hormone ethylene.
Acclimatisation Physiological adjustment to a change in an environmental factor.
Acetylcholine Most common neurotransmitter that functions by binding to receptors and altering the permeability of the post-synaptic membrane to specific ions, either depolarizing or hyperpolarizing the membrane.
Acrosome An organelle at the tip of a sperm cell that helps the sperm penetrate the egg.
Actin A globular multifunctional protein that forms microfilaments.
Active Immunity Immunity resulting from antibodies formed by the human in response to an antigen.
Active Site Specific portion of an enzyme that attaches to the substrate by means of weak chemical bonds.
Active Transport The movement of a substance across the membrane against its concentration gradient with the expenditure of energy and specific transport proteins.
Adaptive Radiation The emergence of numerous species from a common ancestor introduced into an environment, presenting a diversity of new opportunities and problems.
Adolescence Period between childhood and puberty.
ADP Adenosine diphosphate, an important organic compound in metabolism and essential to the flow of energy in living cells.
Adrenaline A hormone produced by the medulla of the adrenal gland that increases glucose concentration in blood, raises blood pressure, and heartbeat.
AMP Adenosine monophosphate, also known as 5'-adenylic acid, a nucleotide used as a monomer in DNA and RNA, produced during ATP synthesis by combining two ADP molecules.
ATP Adenosine triphosphate, a nucleotide triphosphate used in cells as a coenzyme, often called the energy currency of a cell.
Aerobic Refers to the presence of oxygen.
AIDS Acquired Immuno Deficiency Syndrome, a sexual disease caused by HIV, characterized by a reduction of T-cells and appearance of secondary infections.
Aldosterone An adrenal hormone that acts on the distal tubules of the kidney to stimulate reabsorption of sodium (Na⁺) and passive flow of water from the filtrate.
Allele An alternative form of a gene.
Allopatric Speciation A mode of speciation induced when the ancestral population becomes segregated by a geographical barrier.
Allopolyploid A common type of polyploid species resulting from two different species interbreeding and combining their chromosomes.
Alveoli Small air sacs of the lungs through which gaseous exchange takes place between blood and air.
Amniocentesis A technique used for determining genetic abnormalities in a fetus by the presence of certain chemicals or defective fetal cells in the amniotic fluid.
Amphibious Organisms that can live on both land and in water.
Amnion The innermost membrane of extraembryonic membranes, enclosed by a fluid-filled sac in which the embryo is suspended.
Anabolism Set of metabolic pathways that form molecules from smaller units with energy input.
Anaerobic Refers to the absence of oxygen.
Angiosperm A flowering plant that forms seeds inside a protective chamber called an ovary.
Analogous Organs or parts similar in function or appearance but not in origin or development.
Anoxia A condition characterized by an absence of oxygen supply to an organ or tissue.
Antibiotic A substance synthesized by microorganisms that damages or kills other microorganisms, e.g., penicillin, streptomycin, terramycin, neomycin.
Antibody A protein produced by the body’s immune system when it detects foreign substances (antigens) in the blood or tissues.
Antigen A foreign substance (usually protein) that elicits the formation of specific antibodies within an organism.
Aorta The major artery in the blood-circulating system that sends blood to other body tissues.
Apical Dominance Concentration of growth at the tip of a plant shoot where a terminal bud partially inhibits axillary bud growth.
Apoptosis Programmed cell death triggered by signals activating a cascade of suicide proteins in cells destined to die.
Autosomes Chromosomes other than sex chromosomes; humans have 22 pairs, common in both males and females.
Bacteria Simplest, single-celled living organisms found everywhere, constituting a large domain of prokaryotic microorganisms.
Basal Metabolism Amount of energy required by an individual in the resting state for functions like breathing and blood circulation.
Biodiversity Hotspot A relatively small area with an exceptional concentration of species.
Biomass The dry weight of organic matter comprising a group of organisms in a particular habitat.
Biome A large naturally occurring community of flora and fauna occupying a major habitat, e.g., forest or tundra.
Biosphere An artificial structure enclosing a self-contained ecosystem or ecosystems.
Blastocyst A mammalian blastula in which some differentiation of cells has occurred.
Blastula An animal embryo at the early stage of development when it is a hollow ball of cells.
Blood Pressure The hydrostatic force that blood exerts against the wall of a vessel.
Blood Serum Blood plasma without clotting factors.
Blood Sugar The concentration of glucose in the blood, i.e., 100 mg/100 mL.
Blue Baby The appearance produced by some congenital heart defects.
Bundle of His A collection of heart muscle cells specialized for electrical conduction that transmits impulses from the AV node to the apex of the fascicular branches.
Carnivore An animal that eats other animals.
C3 Plant A plant that uses the Calvin cycle for initial steps to incorporate CO₂ into organic material, forming a three-carbon compound as the first stable intermediate.
C4 Plant A plant that prefaces the Calvin cycle with reactions that incorporate CO₂ into four-carbon compounds, supplying CO₂ for the Calvin cycle.
Callus A mass of unorganized parenchyma cells derived from plant tissue (explants) for use in biological research, covering plant wounds.
Calyx Sepals of a flower.
Carcinogen A chemical agent that causes cancer.
Cardiac Output The volume of blood pumped per minute by the left ventricle of the heart.
Casparian Strip A water-impermeable ring of wax around endodermal cells in plants that blocks the passive flow of water and solutes into the stele via cell walls.
Cell Cycle An ordered sequence of events in a dividing eukaryotic cell, involving G₀, G₁, S, G₂, and M phases.
Cell Differentiation The structural and functional divergence of cells as they become specialized during a multicellular organism’s development.
Cellular Respiration An efficient catabolic pathway for ATP production, consuming oxygen as a reactant along with organic fuel.
Cell Sap The solution of inorganic and some organic substances found in the vacuole of a plant cell.
Centriole A small cylindrical cell organelle near the nucleus in animal cells, involved in spindle fiber development during cell division.
Chiasmata The X-shaped, microscopically visible region representing homologous chromatids that have exchanged genetic material through crossing-over during meiosis.
Chromosomes Self-duplicating rod-shaped or thread-like structures of nucleic acid and protein in the nucleus, carrying genetic information in the form of genes.
Chyme Partially digested food after leaving the stomach.
Commensalism An association between two species where one benefits and the other is neither harmed nor benefited.
Conjunctiva The thin mucous membrane that lines the eyelids and the exposed surface of the eyeball.
Consumers Organisms (heterotrophs) incapable of synthesizing their own food, obtaining nourishment by consuming producers or other consumers.
Corpus Callosum A broad band of nerve fibers joining the two hemispheres of the brain.
Corpus Luteum A secreting tissue in the ovary formed from the collapsed follicle after ovulation, producing progesterone.
Cytokinesis The division of the cytoplasm to form two separate daughter cells immediately after mitosis.
Decomposers Organisms that obtain nutrients by feeding on dead organisms.
Deoxyribonucleic Acid (DNA) A double-helix structure consisting of two long chains held together by hydrogen bonds, present in all living organisms.
Desmosome A type of intercellular junction in animal cells that functions as an anchor.
Diabetes Mellitus Inability to metabolize carbohydrates and fats due to insulin deficiency, resulting in glucose excretion through urine.
Dialysis Separation of particles in a liquid based on their ability to pass through a membrane, used for clinical blood purification.
Dicotyledon A flowering plant with an embryo bearing two cotyledons.
Dihybrid A hybrid individual heterozygous for two genes.
Dimorphism Displaying two separate growth forms.
Diffusion Movement of materials from higher to lower concentration.
Ecotype A locally adapted variant of a species, a biological community of interacting organisms and their physical environment.
Embryo The fertilized egg of a vertebrate animal after cleavage.
Endometrium The inner lining of the uterus, richly supplied with blood vessels.
Epistasis The phenomenon where one gene alters the expression of another independently inherited gene.
Evolution The process by which different types of living organisms develop from earlier forms during Earth's history.
Fertilization The process of fusion of a male gamete with a female gamete, resulting in a zygote.
Fibrinogen A soluble protein in blood plasma from which fibrin is produced by thrombin action.
Gastrulation The formulation of a gastrula from a blastula.
Gene The unit of heredity, a segment of DNA in all living organisms or RNA in some RNA viruses.
Glycolysis An anaerobic process breaking down glucose molecules by enzymes, releasing energy and pyruvic acid.
Guttation Secretion of water droplets through hydathodes at the tip and margins of certain leaves.
Haemoglobin Iron-containing, red-colored respiratory pigment that transports oxygen in the blood of vertebrates.
Haploid An organism or structure with a single set of unpaired chromosomes.
Herbivore An organism that feeds on plants.
Heterotrophs Organisms that cannot derive food from complex organic materials.
Heterozygous Organisms with dissimilar alleles at corresponding chromosomal loci, producing dissimilar gametes (e.g., Rt, Tt, Ww).
Homeostasis The tendency to maintain stability in the internal environment without changing the external environment.
Homologous Structures or organs of various animals with similarity in basic structure and development but not necessarily in function.
Homozygous An individual with identical alleles at corresponding chromosomal loci (e.g., RR, rr, tt).
Human Genome Project An international effort to map and sequence the entire human genome.
Host An organism (plant, animal, or microbe) on which another organism (parasite) feeds.
Hydrolysis Breakdown of complex molecules into simpler ones using water.
Hydroponics The process of growing plants in sand, gravel, or aqueous solutions containing essential mineral elements, without soil.
Hymen A fold of mucous membrane that normally partially covers the vaginal opening.
Immunity The tendency of a body to be safe from getting a disease.
Insectivore An animal that eats mainly insects.
Karyogamy Fusion of nuclei of two cells as part of syngamy.
Karyotype A method of organizing chromosomes of a cell by number, size, and type.
Lactation Process of milk secretion by the mammary glands.
Lenticels Minute pores raised through the cork of a woody stem or root for gaseous exchange between the atmosphere and internal tissues.
Leukemia Blood cancer characterized by a rapid, abnormal increase in white blood cells, including many immature cells.
Lichen A symbiotic relationship between an alga and a fungus, often growing on rocks and tree trunks.
Meiocyte A cell that differentiates into a gamete through meiosis.
Meiosis A type of cell division resulting in four daughter cells with half the chromosomes of the parent cell, used in gamete and spore production.
Menarche The first occurrence of menstruation in girls.
Meninges Membranes enclosing the brain and spinal cord.
Mesosomes Special structures formed by infoldings of the bacterial membrane, increasing its surface area.
Metabolism Chemical processes in a living organism to maintain itself and carry out characteristic activities.
Metastasis Transfer of disease from one organ or body part to another not connected to it, significant in cancer.
Metamorphosis A complete change in shape and appearance of an organism during development, e.g., caterpillar to pupa.
Mitosis Type of cell division where chromosomes split into two daughter cells with the same number and kind of chromosomes as the parent nucleus.
Monocarpic A plant that flowers once in its life and then dies, e.g., century plant (Agave).
Monosomic A cell or individual that is basically diploid but has one copy of a particular chromosome (2n-1).
Mutation A sudden, discrete change in the genome, creating a new character or trait not found in the parent.
Migration Seasonal movement of animals from one region to another.
Monocotyledon A flowering plant with an embryo bearing a single cotyledon (seed leaf).
Mutualism A relationship between two or more species where both participants benefit.
Myocardial Infarction A heart attack caused by the blockage of a coronary artery.
NADP Nicotinamide Adenine Dinucleotide Phosphate, a coenzyme functioning as an electron carrier in processes like photosynthesis and respiration.
Nerve Impulse Transmission of a signal distally along the length of an axon (nerve fiber).
Nitrification Process of converting ammonia into nitrites (NO₂⁻) and nitrates (NO₃⁻).
Nucleic Acid Macromolecule composed of many nucleotides linked in a long chain, e.g., DNA or RNA.
Nucleoid Nuclear material of bacteria.
Omnivore An organism that feeds on both plant and animal material.
Ovule The part of the ovary of seed plants containing the female germ cells, becoming the seed after fertilization.
Parasite An organism that lives in or on another organism (host).
Parthenocarpy Development of fruits without seed formation.
Passive Immunity Temporary protection against an infectious disease by injecting antibodies from an animal or human exposed to the disease.
Pathogen Any organism that causes disease.
Peristalsis Involuntary contraction and relaxation of muscles in the walls of hollow, tubular organs (e.g., digestive tract, uterus) to move contents through.
Phagocytosis The engulfing of microorganisms, other cells, and foreign particles by phagocytes and amoeboid protozoans.
Plasma Membrane A delicate membranous layer of lipids and proteins, selectively permeable in nature.
Pleura The membrane that envelops the lungs and lines the walls of the chest and diaphragm.
Pseudopodia Temporary protrusion of protoplasm or any cell, usually functioning in locomotion and feeding.
Puberty The period when gonads become functional, and an individual becomes sexually mature.
Purines Nitrogenous bases (adenine and guanine) found in RNA and DNA.
Radicle The embryonic part of a plant that develops into the primary roots.
Respiration Enzyme-controlled oxidation reaction breaking down organic molecules like carbohydrates and fats, releasing energy for cell/organism activities.
Recessive Gene A gene phenotypically expressed in the homozygous form but weakened in the presence of a dominant gene.
Ribonucleic Acid (RNA) A single-helix structure of ribonucleotides (ribose, phosphoric acid, nitrogenous base with uracil replacing thymine).
Ribosomal RNA (rRNA) An essential component for protein synthesis in all living organisms.
Ruminants Mammals that acquire nutrients from plant-based food by fermenting it in a specialized stomach through bacterial action.
Skeleton The supporting framework of bone, cartilage, or other rigid material that supports and protects the soft parts of the body.
Species A group of similar organisms that can breed and produce more of their own kind, the basic unit of classification.
Sperm Male gamete or sex cell in mammals, including humans.
Spore Reproductive unit of certain plants capable of giving rise to a new individual.
Stamen The male fertilizing organ of a flower, the pollen-bearing part.
Sensory Neuron A nerve cell that receives information from the internal and external environment and transmits signals to the CNS.
Septum A partition that divides a structure, such as a fungal hypha, into compartments.
Sinoatrial (SA) Node Area of the vertebrate heart that initiates the heartbeat, located where the superior vena cava enters the right atrium.
Somatic Cells Any cell in a multicellular organism except a sperm or egg cell.
Thermoregulation The maintenance of internal temperature within a tolerable range.
Ti-plasmid A plasmid of a tumor-inducing bacterium that integrates a segment of its DNA into the host chromosome of a plant, used as a carrier for genetic engineering in plants.
Thrombin An enzyme derived from prothrombin, responsible for converting fibrinogen to fibrin in blood clotting.
Thromboplastin An enzyme released from damaged cells that converts prothrombin (inactive) into thrombin (active).
Toxoid A chemically modified toxin treated to decrease toxicity while allowing antibody production, used as a vaccine.
Totipotency Ability of embryonic cells to retain the potential to form all parts of the animal.
Transpiration Loss of water droplets from the aerial parts of a plant, mainly through leaves.
Uterus A female reproductive organ where eggs are fertilized and/or development of the young occurs.
Vaccine An antigenic substance derived from a disease-causing agent, administered to establish resistance to infection.
Vector An organism that transmits pathogens or parasites from one animal or plant to another.
Vascular Cambium A continuous cylinder of meristematic cells surrounding the xylem and pith, producing secondary xylem and phloem.
Vegetative Propagation Cloning of plants by asexual means.
Venule A very small vein.
Vivipary Reproduction inside the mother’s body, leading to live birth as opposed to laying eggs.
Water Potential Physical property predicting the direction of water flow, governed by solute concentration and applied pressure.
Xylem Tube-shaped, non-living portion of the vascular system in plants that carries water and minerals from roots to the rest of the plant.
Yolk Stored food in egg cells that nourishes the egg.
Yolk Sac One of four extraembryonic membranes that supports embryonic development.
Zooplankton Non-photosynthetic organisms present in plankton.
Zygote The diploid product formed by the union of haploid gametes in conception, i.e., a fertilized egg.
- Biology: Diversity in Living World - Overview of Living Organisms - Definition: - All living things are identified and categorized based on their body forms and functions. - Some characteristics cause more significant changes in body design than others, influencing classification. - Systematics: - Definition: - The scientific study aimed at recognizing, describing, naming, and arranging diverse organisms according to an organized plan based on unique species and group features. - Components: - Identification: - Determining the correct name and position of an organism within an established classification plan. - Classification: - Arranging organisms into groups based on similarities and differences. - Nomenclature: - A system for naming organisms. - Taxonomy: - Framing rules for classification, with identification, classification, and nomenclature as foundational steps. - Classification - Definition: - Scientific arrangement of organisms in a hierarchical series of groups based on similarities and differences. - Basis of Classification: - Characteristics that distinguish certain groups of organisms from others, such as habitat, blood color, or reproductive methods (oviparous or viviparous). - Aristotle’s Contribution: - Known as the father of biology and zoology, Aristotle classified animals based on: - Habitat (land, water, air). - Blood color (red or not). - Reproduction (oviparous or viviparous). - Objectives of Classification: - Recognize and distribute basic units (species) based on pre-decided characteristics. - Devise criteria for assigning species into an ascending series of groups based on similarities and relationships. - Illustrate evolutionary changes in organisms. - History of Classification - Key Historical Developments: - Early classification into viviparous, oviparous, and minute animals. - Hippocrates and Aristotle grouped animals into birds, insects, fishes, and whales. - Pliny the Elder introduced artificial classification, listing 1000 economic plants in *Historia Naturalis*. - John Ray described over 18,000 plants and animals in *Historia Generalis Plantarum* and introduced the term “species” for morphologically similar organisms. - Carolus Linnaeus: - Developed the binomial system of nomenclature. - Described 5,900 plant species in *Species Plantarum* and 4,326 animal species in *Systema Naturae* and *Philosophia Botanica*. - Classified organisms into two kingdoms: Plantae and Animalia in *Systema Naturae*. - Known as the Father of Taxonomy for creating a uniform system for naming plants and animals. - Evolution of Systematics: - From Aristotle to Linnaeus, classification evolved from classical taxonomy to natural systems, numerical taxonomy (phenetics), and phylogenetic classification (cladistics). - Hierarchy of Classification - - Definition: - A hierarchical system introduced by Linnaeus, later expanded by biologists like Ernst Haeckel (1894), Robert Whittaker (1958), and Carl Woese (1977), to categorize organisms into broad kingdoms. - Major Categories: - Kingdom (Highest) - Phylum (for animals) / Division (for plants) - Class - Order - Family - Genus - Species (Lowest) - Basis: - Organisms are separated from larger, broader groups to smaller, specific groups, with species as the basic unit of classification. - Binomial System of Nomenclature - Definition: - A scientific naming system devised by Carolus Linnaeus, where each organism is given a two-word name: - First word: - Generic name (genus). - Second word: - Specific name (species). - Example: - \( \text{Canis familiaris} \) for dog. - Examples of Common and Scientific Names: -
Common NameScientific Name

- Categories of Classification - Two Kingdom Classification: - Proposed by Carolus Linnaeus (1758), dividing organisms into Plantae and Animalia. - Four Kingdom Classification: - Proposed by Copeland (1956), including Monera (bacteria, blue-green algae), Protista (red and brown algae, fungi, Protozoa), Plantae, and Animalia. - Five Kingdom Classification: - Proposed by R.H. Whittaker (1969), based on cell structure, body organization, nutrition mode, reproduction, and ancestral relationships. - Kingdoms: - Monera, Protista, Fungi, Plantae, Animalia. - Comparison of Five Kingdoms: -
FeaturesMoneraProtistaFungiPlantaeAnimalia

- Viruses - Definition: - Term coined by Pasteur; discovered by Ivanowski in tobacco plants with mosaic disease. - Recovered in crystalline form by Stanley (Nobel Prize, 1946). - Studied in virology; intracellular parasites lacking enzymes for energy generation. - Characteristics: - Considered non-living outside hosts but act as a link between living and non-living. - Dependent on hosts for survival; exist as crystals outside hosts. - Cause dangerous infections when inside hosts. - Composed of ~94% protein, ~6% nucleic acid (DNA or RNA, single/double-stranded) covered by a protein coat (capsid) made of capsomeres. - Size: - 10–300 mµ; smallest virus: 20 mµ; largest: pox virus (300 mµ). - Shapes: - Isodiametric, spherical, polygonal, anisodiametric, rod-shaped, or thread-like. - Bacteriophages: - Bacteria-infecting viruses discovered by Frederick Twort and Felix d’Herelle. - Kingdom Monera - Definition: - Proposed by Ernst Haeckel (1866); includes small, simple, microscopic prokaryotes (e.g., Archaebacteria, Eubacteria). - Mostly unicellular, lacking a true nucleus and organelles. - General Characteristics: - Unicellular, without membrane-bound nucleus. - Reproduce asexually via binary fission or sporulation. - Nutrition: - Autotrophic (synthesize own food) or heterotrophic (depend on others). - Eubacteria (True Bacteria): - Lack a true nucleus; include bacteria, cyanobacteria (blue-green algae), mycoplasma, actinomycetes, rickettsia. - Bacteria: - Discovered by Antony van Leeuwenhoek (1683); named by Ehrenberg (1829). - Unicellular, ubiquitous, found in water, soil, organisms; vary in shape and size. - Shapes: - Coccus (spherical/oval), bacillus (rod), spirillum (spirally coiled), vibrio (comma-shaped), stalked, budding, mycelial (filamentous). - General Characteristics of Bacteria: - Thick cell wall (chitin) and inner membrane (proteins, phospholipids). - Nutrition: - Autotrophic (photosynthetic/chemosynthetic) or heterotrophic (saprophytic, symbiotic, parasitic). - Reproduction: - Asexual (binary fission); parasexual (conjugation, transformation). - Movement: - Some have flagella for rhythmic movement. - Respiration: - Via mesosomes. - Examples: - Methanogenic bacteria (e.g., Methanococcus produce methane from formic acid); halophilic bacteria (e.g., Halococcus thrive in salt-rich media). - Spore-producing bacteria: - Gram-positive, purple on staining, cell walls composed of murein. - Mycoplasma: - Smallest living cells, lack cell wall, aerobic, non-motile, pleomorphic, prokaryotic, contain RNA and DNA. - Known as PPLO (pleuropneumonia-like organisms). - Economic Importance of Bacteria: -
ActivityBacteriaDescription
Vinegar ProductionAcetobacter acetiOxidizes ethyl alcohol from molasses into acetic acid.
Food PoisoningClostridium botulinumCauses botulism, accompanied by diarrhea and vomiting.
NitrificationNitrosomonas, Nitrococcus, Nitrobacter, NitrocystisOxidize ammonium salts to nitrites, then nitrates.
DenitrificationThiobacillus denitrificansConverts soil nitrates to gaseous nitrogen.
Natural ScavengersSaprotrophic bacteriaDecompose dead plants/animals and organic sewage content.
DiseasesVarious bacteriaCause >90% of human/animal diseases, >40% of plant diseases.
Biofertilizers/ManureVarious bacteriaConvert dung/organic waste into manure and methane.
Food SpoilageSaprotrophic bacteriaCause rotting of vegetables, fruits, meat, bread.
DesulphurificationDesulfovibrio desulfuricansConverts soil sulphates to \( \text{H}_2\text{S} \).
- Pasteurization of Milk: - Developed by Louis Pasteur; kills harmful bacteria without altering milk’s content or flavor. - Methods: - Low Temperature Long Time (LTLT): - Heat at \( 63^\circ \text{C} \) for 30 minutes, then cool quickly. - High Temperature Short Time (HTST): - Heat at \( 72^\circ \text{C} \) for 15 seconds, then cool quickly. - Major Types of Bacteria: - Actinomycetes: - Gram-positive, branching filaments, produce antibiotics (e.g., Streptomyces, Actinomyces). - Cyanobacteria: - Photosynthetic, gram-negative, cause blooms in polluted water (e.g., Anabaena, Nostoc). - Rickettsia: - Small, gram-negative, intracellular parasites, cause diseases like typhus (e.g., Rickettsia, Chlamydia). - Archaebacteria: - Primitive, live in extreme habitats; include methanogens (e.g., Methanobacterium), halophiles (e.g., Halobacterium), thermoacidophiles (e.g., Sulfolobus). - Kingdom Protista - Definition: - Proposed by Ernst Haeckel (1886); includes aquatic, unicellular, eukaryotic organisms, evolved ~1000 million years ago. - Major Groups: - Photosynthetic Protists: - Unicellular algae (e.g., diatoms, euglenoids). - Protozoan Protists: - Holozoic, saprozoic, or parasitic (e.g., Trypanosoma, Plasmodium, Paramecium). - Consumer-Decomposer Protists: - Lack chlorophyll, feed on decaying matter (e.g., slime molds). - General Characteristics of Protozoa: - Size: - 10–52 µm, up to 1 mm. - Movement: - Via flagella (5–10 µm), cilia (20–30 µm), or pseudopodia (2–20 µm thick). - Digestion: - Intracellular in vacuoles. - Nutrition: - Saprophytic, parasitic, symbiotic, or holozoic. - Respiration/Excretion: - Via diffusion through body surface. - Reproduction: - Asexual and sexual. - Examples: - Amoeba, E. coli, Leishmania donovani, Plasmodium, Euglena. - General Characteristics of Protista: - Cell membrane: - Plasmalemma. - Cell wall: - Cellulose, protein strips (pellicle), or silica. - Organelles: - Mitochondria, Golgi body, endoplasmic reticulum, nucleus, chromosomes. - Ribosomes: - 80S. - Chloroplasts: - Present in photosynthetic forms (e.g., dinoflagellates). - Reproduction: - Sexual and asexual (binary fission). - Locomotion: - Pseudopodial (e.g., Amoeba), flagellar, ciliary; flagella/cilia have 9+2 fibrils. - Nutrition: - Autotrophic, heterotrophic, or both. - Notes: - Cellulose-digesting protists found in termites and wood-eating animals. - Gonyaulax causes red tide. - Diatoms (e.g., Navicula) glide toward light. - Pathogenic protists (e.g., Amoeba, Plasmodium, Trypanosoma) cause diarrhea, malaria, African sleeping sickness. - Economic Importance of Protista: -
RoleDescriptionExamples
PathogensCause diseases in humans/plantsVarious protists

- Kingdom Fungi - Definition: - Primitive, eukaryotic thallophytes (lacking roots, stems, leaves); unicellular or multicellular; definite cell wall, true nucleus, no chlorophyll. - Types Based on Nutrition: - Saprophytic: - Feed on decayed plants/animals, organic residues (e.g., Rhizopus, Penicillium). - Parasitic: - Feed on living organisms (e.g., Puccinia, Ustilago). - Symbiotic: - Mutualistic associations (e.g., lichens with alga and fungus). - General Characteristics: - Structure: - Body of hyphae (tubular filaments) forming mycelium. - Cell Wall: - Chitin or fungal cellulose. - Food Reserve: - Glycogen and oil. - Reproduction: - Sexual: - Fusion of gametes or nuclei. - Asexual: - Via spores (e.g., Mucor, Rhizopus). - Vegetative: - Fragmentation, budding, fission. - Examples: - Yeast, mushrooms, Aspergillus, Penicillium, Rhizopus, Agaricus. - Economic Importance of Fungi: -
AdvantagesDescriptionExamples
Soil FertilityDecompose organic matter into manureRhizopus
Nitrogen FixationEnhances soil fertilityRhodotorula
MedicineAntibiotics (e.g., penicillin from Penicillium notatum)Chloromycetin, neomycin, streptomycin

Vitamin SynthesisVitamin B12, D, B2Streptomyces griseus, Ashbya gossypii
Enzyme FormationAmylase, invertase, pectinaseAspergillus oryzae, yeast, Penicillium

DisadvantagesDescriptionExamples

Wood DecayDecompose woodPoria, Phomis, Merulius
DiseasesCause plant/animal/human diseases (e.g., leaf curl, ringworm, baldness)Taphrina deformans, Microsporum, Taenia capitis
- Lichens: - Definition: - Symbiotic organisms of fungi (mycobiont) and algae (phycobiont). - Characteristics: - Mutualistic relationship; fungi typically Ascomycota. - Found in hot deserts, rocky coasts, toxic slag heaps, rainforests. - Reproduction: - Asexual, sexual, or vegetative. - Uses: - Food: - Staple or delicacy (e.g., Iceland moss, Parmelia); some are poisonous. - Dyes: - Violet from orchil, laconora. - Environmental: - Accumulate pollutants (e.g., lead, copper). - Medicinal: - Used in epilepsy treatment, antibiotics (e.g., usenic acid from Usnea). - Perfumes: - Used in manufacturing. - Bioindicators: - Indicate air pollution (e.g., sulphur dioxide). - Kingdom Plantae - - Definition: - Includes green, brown, red algae, liverworts, mosses, ferns, seed plants (with/without flowers). - Multicellular, eukaryotic, chlorophyll-containing, with cellulosic cell walls. - Classification: - Thallophyta: - No differentiated plant body (e.g., algae). - Bryophyta: - No specialized vascular tissue. - Tracheophyta: - With vascular tissues (Pteridophyta, Gymnosperms, Angiosperms). - Pteridophyta: - Seedless. - Gymnosperms: - Naked seeds. - Angiosperms: - Seeds in fruits (Monocotyledonae, Dicotyledonae). - Thallophyta (Algae): - Definition: - Chlorophyll-bearing, photosynthetic, autotrophic, thalloid plants; no true roots, stems, leaves. - Habitats: - Freshwater, marine, wet rocks, soil, tree trunks. - Forms: - Filamentous (e.g., Spirogyra), colonial (e.g., Volvox). - Cellular Structure: - Eukaryotic, cell wall with cellulose, nucleus, cytoplasm with chloroplasts, mitochondria, Golgi body. - Types Based on Pigments/Food/Flagellation: -
TypeCharacteristicsExamples

Red AlgaePhycoerythrin, marine, multicellular, cellulose/pectin cell wall, chlorophyll a, carotenes, xanthophylls, phycobilins, floridean starchPorphyra, Gracilaria
- Notes: - Cryptophytes: - Grow on ice. - Lithophytes: - Grow on rocks. - Reproduction: - Vegetative: - Fragmentation, budding, harmogonia (e.g., Spirogyra, Sphacelaria). - Asexual: - Zoospores, akinetes, aplanospores, autospores, monospores, bispores, polyspores, endospores, hypnospores (e.g., Ulothrix, Vaucheria). - Sexual: - Isogamy, anisogamy, oogamy (e.g., Spirogyra, Chlamydomonas, Volvox). - Economic Importance: -

- Everyday Science: - Blue-green algae cause greenish ground surfaces. - Phycocolloids from red algae used in ice creams, chocolates, sauces, toothpastes, cosmetics. - Chara (stonewort) destroys mosquito larvae. - Bromine from Rhodomela, Polysiphonia; iodine from Laminaria. - Bryophyta: - Definition: - Simplest terrestrial, non-vascular, autotrophic, non-flowering, non-seeded plants; amphibians of plant kingdom. - Examples: - Anthoceros, Polytrichum, Funaria, Marchantia. - General Characteristics: - Plant body: - Thallus-like, attached by rhizoids; differentiated into stem/leaf-like structures. - Main body: - Haploid gametophyte producing gametes. - No vascular tissues (xylem, phloem). - Monoecious; male (antheridium) and female (archegonium) sex organs covered by sterile jacket. - Nutrition: - Autotrophic, with chlorophyll; reserve food is starch. - Cell wall: - Cellulose with pectic layer (galacturonic acid). - Reproduction: - Oogamous (sexual), vegetative. - Limitation: - Cannot grow >70–80 cm due to lack of roots, vascular tissues, cuticle, mechanical tissues. - Economic Importance: -
AdvantagesDescriptionExamples
Soil Erosion PreventionHigh water absorption capacityMosses
Soil FormationContribute to plant succession in rocky areasSphagnum
PeatUsed as fuel, lamp wicks; acidic, hygroscopicSphagnum
AntisepticsUsed as antisepticsMosses
- Tracheophyta: - Definition: - Plants with vascular tissues (xylem, phloem) for conduction; ~275,000 species. - Subgroups: - Pteridophyta, Gymnosperms, Angiosperms. - Pteridophyta: - Definition: - Seedless vascular plants (cryptogams) reproducing via spores; coined by Haeckel (1866); ~13,000 species. - Examples: - Selaginella, Salvinia, Marsilea. - Characteristics: - Found in cool, damp, shady places or sandy soils. - Size: - Fraction of cm (e.g., Azolla) to 20 m (tree ferns). - Plant body: - Differentiated into roots, stems, leaves. - Vascular tissues: - Xylem, phloem. - Main body: - Sporophyte; spores in sporangia on sporophylls. - Sex organs: - Antheridium (male), archegonium (female). - Alternation of generations. - Economic Importance: -
RoleDescriptionExamples
FodderUsed for domestic animalsPteridium
MedicineMedicinal seedsLycopodium
CommercialGold extractionEquisetum
OrnamentalGrown for graceful appearanceFerns
- Gymnosperms: - Definition: - Plants with naked seeds (no ovary); ~900 species; found in colder northern hemispheres. - Examples: - Pinus, Cycas. - Characteristics: - Sporophytic body; differentiated into roots, stems, leaves. - Roots: - Tap roots; some with mycorrhiza (e.g., Pinus). - Stems: - Branched (Pinus) or unbranched (Cycas). - Perennial, woody; wood monoxylic (Cycas) or pycnoxylic (Pinus). - Pollination: - Anemophilous (wind). - Polyembryony tendency. - Vascular tissues in bundles; no flowers; sporophylls form cones (pollen/seed). - Economic Importance: -

Tanning/ResinUsed in leather, ink, shoe polish, plastics, paintsVarious gymnosperms
Decorative/DomesticRopes, broomsCycas
- Angiosperms: - Definition: - Seed-bearing plants with seeds enclosed in ovary (fruits); ~250,000 species; evolved 130–160 million years ago. - Habitats: - Land, Himalayas (up to 6000 m), Antarctica, Tundra. - Characteristics: - Leaves: - Simple or compound. - Sex organs: - Stamen (male), pistil/carpel (female). - Double fertilization; triploid (\( 3n \)) endosperm. - Vascular tissues present. - Nutrition: - Saprophytic, symbiotic, parasitic, or autotrophic. - Classification: - Monocotyledons (Monocots): - Single cotyledon. - Features: - Dumbbell-shaped stomata, no secondary growth, floral parts in threes, no cambium, scattered/closed vascular bundles. - Examples: - Grasses, bamboo, sugarcane, cereals, bananas, palms, lilies, orchids, onion. - Dicotyledons (Dicots): - Two cotyledons. - Features: - Kidney-shaped stomata, roots from radicle, secondary growth, floral parts in fours/fives, cambium present, open/ringed vascular bundles. - Examples: - Hardwood trees, pulses, fruits, vegetables, spices. - Economic Importance: - Major food, fiber, spice, beverage crops. - Used in medicines, perfumes, rubber, soaps, cosmetics. - Provide timber and medicines. - Notes: - Sequoia sempervirens: - Tallest gymnosperm. - Zamia pygmaea: - Smallest gymnosperm. - Cycas: - Most primitive gymnosperm. - Sequoia gigantea: - Known as father of forest. - Kingdom Animalia - Definition: - Includes ~1 million described species, from sponges to mammals; multicellular, heterotrophic, eukaryotic. - General Characteristics: - No cell walls. - Levels of Organization: - Protoplasmic: - Protozoans. - Cellular: - Sponges, mesozoans. - Tissue: - Platyhelminthes. - Organ system: - Higher invertebrates, vertebrates. - Body Symmetry: - Asymmetrical: - Divisible into equal halves by any plane (e.g., Amoeba, some sponges). - Radial: - Equal halves through longitudinal axis (e.g., Porifera, Coelenterata, Ctenophora, Echinodermata). - Bilateral: - Divided into right/left halves by sagittal plane (e.g., Platyhelminthes, Nematoda, Annelida, Mollusca, Arthropoda, Chordata). - Coelom: - Coelomates: - Body cavity lined by mesoderm (e.g., annelids, molluscs, arthropods, echinoderms, hemichordates, chordates). - Pseudocoelomates: - Cavity not lined by mesoderm (e.g., nematodes). - Acoelomates: - No coelom (e.g., sponges, coelenterates, Platyhelminthes). - Classification: - Phylum Porifera (Sponges): - Definition: - Primitive multicellular animals with pores (ostia) forming a canal system for water flow; studied by RE Grant (1825). - Characteristics: - Hard skeletal covering; spherical or irregular shape. - No distinct tissues/organs; mostly marine, some freshwater, solitary/colonial, sessile. - Loosely arranged cells, not true diploblastic. - Sac-like, cylindrical, tubular, or cushion-shaped body. - Intracellular digestion; hermaphrodite with cross-fertilization. - Reproduction: - Asexual and sexual. - Examples: - Sycon, Spongilla, Euspongia. - Phylum Coelenterata (Cnidaria): - Definition: - ~9,000 species; named for cnidoblasts (stinging cells); lower invertebrates with radial symmetry. - Characteristics: - Aquatic, mostly marine, some freshwater (e.g., Hydra). - Radial or biradial symmetry; cell-tissue grade organization. - Diploblastic (epidermis, gastrodermis). - Polymorphic: - Polyp (asexual), medusa (sexual). - Exoskeleton: - Chitinous (perisarc) or calcareous (corals). - Examples: - Physalia, Adamsia, Pennatula, Gorgonia, Meandrina, Hydra, Aurelia. - Phylum Ctenophora: - Definition: - Small phylum (~50 species) of marine comb jellies/sea walnuts. - Characteristics: - Free-swimming via cilia; diploblastic, soft, transparent, gelatinous. - Eight ciliary comb plates. - Digestion: - Intra- and extracellular. - Examples: - Pleurobrachia, Ctenoplana. - Phylum Platyhelminthes (Flatworms): - Definition: - Primitive, bilaterally symmetrical, acoelomate, triploblastic worms. - Characteristics: - Free-living, commensal, or parasitic; leaf/ribbon-like body. - Tissue-organ grade organization. - Adhesive structures (hooks, spines, suckers) in parasitic forms. - Flattened dorsoventrally; incomplete digestive system (no anus). - Monoecious; internal fertilization (self/cross). - Examples: - Taenia, Fasciola. - Phylum Aschelminthes/Nematoda (Roundworms): - Definition: - Aquatic, free-living, or parasitic worms with pseudocoelom. - Characteristics: - Bilaterally symmetrical, triploblastic. - Complete digestive canal (mouth, pharynx, intestine, anus). - No circulatory/respiratory systems; nervous system with cerebral ganglia. - Parasitic forms cause diseases (e.g., elephantiasis). - Examples: - Ascaris, Wuchereria. - Phylum Annelida: - Definition: - Elongated, bilaterally symmetrical, triploblastic, coelomate, metamerically segmented worms. - Characteristics: - Aquatic or terrestrial; some commensal/parasitic. - True coelom; well-developed digestive system. - Excretory organs: - Kidneys; locomotory organs: Setae/chaetae. - Closed blood vascular system with hemoglobin in RBCs. - Respiration: - Moist skin, gills, parapodia. - Examples: - Nereis, Aphrodita, Pheretima, Hirudinea. - Phylum Arthropoda: - Definition: - Largest animal phylum; bilaterally symmetrical, triploblastic, metamerically segmented. - Characteristics: - Organ-system organization; jointed appendages (jaws, gills, legs). - Complete digestive system; respiration via body surface, gills, tracheae, book-lungs. - Open circulatory system; excretory organs: Green glands, Malpighian tubules. - Nervous system: - Dorsal brain, nerve ring; sensory organs: Eyes, statocysts, taste/sound receptors. - Dioecious. - Examples: - Apis, Bombyx, Luccifer, Anopheles, Culex, Aedes, Locusta, Limulus. - Everyday Science: - Cockroaches cannot survive in water due to tracheal respiration. - Cockroach larvae: - Maggots. - Ants have compound eyes for all-around vision. - Phylum Mollusca: - Definition: - Second-largest phylum (~85,000 species); soft-bodied invertebrates; coined by Johnston (1650). - Characteristics: - Mostly marine, some freshwater/terrestrial. - Mantle covers body, enclosing pallial cavity. - Complete digestive system with hepatopancreas, radula. - Excretory system: - Kidneys (ammonia/uric acid). - Respiration: - Gills (ctenidia), mantle. - Locomotion: - Muscular foot. - Mostly dioecious; some hermaphrodite. - Examples: - Pila, Pinctada, Sepia. - Phylum Echinodermata: - Definition: - Spiny-skinned marine animals (~6,000 species); pentamerous radial symmetry. - Characteristics: - Organ-system organization; triploblastic, coelomate. - Bilateral symmetry in larvae; pentamerous radial in adults. - Spines, pedicellariae for protection. - Complete digestive tract; open vascular system (haemal, perihaemal). - Respiration: - Dermal branchiae, tube feet, respiratory tree, bursae. - No brain or excretory organs. - Examples: - Asterias, Echinus, Antedon, Cucumaria, Ophicura. - Phylum Hemichordata: - Definition: - Small group of marine, worm-like animals; previously under Chordata. - Characteristics: - Live in burrows; true coelom. - Complete digestive system; open circulatory system. - Sensory cells in epidermis; mostly sexual reproduction. - Respiration: - Gill slits or body surface. - Examples: - Balanoglossus, Cephalodiscus. - Phylum Chordata: - Definition: - Most advanced animal phylum; created by Balfour (1880). - Characteristics: - Notochord for support; persists in lower chordates. - Dorsal tubular nerve cord from ectoderm. - Closed circulatory system; pharyngeal slits (modified in higher forms). - Fully developed nervous, digestive, excretory, reproductive systems. - Bilateral symmetry, triploblastic, coelomate, organ-system organization. - Subdivisions: - Protochordates (Urochordata, Cephalochordata): - Primitive, marine, no vertebral column. - Bilaterally symmetrical, triploblastic, coelomate. - Notochord present in some life stages. - Examples: - Balanoglossus, Herdmania, Amphioxus. - Vertebrates: - Higher chordates with vertebral column, internal skeleton. - Four regions: - Head, neck, trunk, tail (neck/tail absent in some, e.g., frogs). - Complex tissue/organ differentiation; complete functional systems. - Classes: - Pisces: - Aquatic; exoskeleton of scales/plates; pectoral/pelvic fins, muscular tail. - Two-chambered heart; gill respiration; air bladder for buoyancy. - Cold-blooded; oviparous. - Subclasses: - Cyclostomata (e.g., Petromyzon, Myxine), Chondrichthyes (e.g., Scoliodon, Pristis), Osteichthyes (e.g., Labeo, Hippocampus). - Amphibia: - Live in water (larvae) and on land (adults); no scales, mucus glands in skin. - Bony endoskeleton; large mouth, mucous-coated tongue. - Respiration: - Gills (larvae), lungs/skin (adults); three-chambered heart. - Cold-blooded; oviparous; ammonotelic. - Examples: - Salamander, Rana tigrina, Hyla, Ecothophis. - Reptilia: - Fully adapted for land; rough, dry skin with horny scales; bony endoskeleton. - Crawl for movement; cold-blooded; lung respiration; oviparous (eggs with calcium carbonate). - Three-chambered heart (four in crocodiles/alligators). - Examples: - Cobra, Draco, Hemidactylus. - Aves: - Birds; feather coat; most fly (except ostrich, emu, etc.). - Modified hind limbs (hopping, grasping); forelimbs for flight. - Thin, dry skin; light, bony endoskeleton; four-chambered heart. - Warm-blooded; uricotelic; oviparous. - Examples: - Ciconia ciconia, Aythya fuligula, Struthio camelus, Apteryx. - Mammalia: - Skin with sweat, oil, milk glands, hair; variable body shape (head, neck, trunk, tail). - Two pairs of limbs; bony endoskeleton; small mouth with lips, teeth. - Complete digestive system; lung respiration; four-chambered heart. - Homeothermic; dioecious. - Subclasses: - Prototheria: - Oviparous, mammary glands without teats (e.g., Platypus, Echidna). - Theria: - Metatheria: - Viviparous, marsupial pouch (e.g., Macropus, Didelphis). - Eutheria: - Nourish fetus via placenta (e.g., humans, cats, rats, bats, tigers). - Examples: - Cat, human, rat, bat. - Everyday Science: - Smallest mammal: - Shrew (<4.5 cm, 2 g). - Dolphin: - Highly intelligent. - Blue whale: - Largest animal. - Echidna: - Lacks corpus callosum. - Atlas: - First vertebra for head movement. - Amphibians: - First vertebrates with true tongue (except Ichthyophis). - Snakes: - No limbs/ears, use Jacobson’s organs to smell. - Dinosaurs: - Fossil reptiles. - Turtles/Tortoises: - Retract head/limbs into shell. - Ostrich: - Largest, fastest-running bird; lays largest egg. - Hummingbird: - Smallest, fastest-flying, only backward-flying bird. - Sunbird: - Smallest Indian bird. - Flightless birds: - Ostrich, Emu, Kakapo, penguin. - Cell: Structure and Functions - Definition - The cell (from Latin *cella*, meaning ‘little room’) is the basic structural, functional, and biological unit of all living organisms, analogous to bricks in a building. - Cells assemble to form the body of every organism. - Discovery of the Cell - Robert Hooke (1665): - First observed cells in a cork slice using a primitive microscope; noted these were dead cells. - A.V. Leeuwenhoek (1674): - Observed living cells in pond water using a compound microscope. - Shape and Size of Cells - Variability: - Cells vary in shape and size across organisms and within organs of the same organism. - Common Shapes: - Round, spherical, elongated, spindle-shaped (long and pointed at both ends), or long (e.g., nerve cells). - Functional Relation: - Shape and size are related to specific functions; some cells have fixed, peculiar shapes (e.g., nerve cells). - Notable Examples: - Smallest cell: - Mycoplasma (PPLO, Pleuro Pneumonia Like Organism), 0.1–0.5 µm. - Largest cell: - Ostrich egg, 170 mm × 130 mm. - Longest cell: - Nerve cell in animals. - Note: - Cork is a substance from tree bark. - Number of Cells in Living Organisms - Unicellular Organisms: - Single-celled, independent organisms (e.g., Amoeba, Chlamydomonas, Paramecium, bacteria). - Multicellular Organisms: - Composed of multiple cells (e.g., fungi, plants, animals). - Origin: - All multicellular organisms originate from a single cell. - Differentiation: - Cells divide to produce specialized cells (e.g., blood, nerve, fat, sperm, bone, ovum, smooth muscle cells) for synchronized organ functions. - Characteristics of Cells - Independent Existence: - Capable of independent replication. - Hereditary Information: - Contains genetic material passed from one generation to another. - Functional Capacity: - Performs basic functions based on location. - Cell Number: - Varies with organism size; small organisms have fewer cells, large animals (e.g., bear, whale) have more. - Cell Theory - Overview: - A fundamental biological generalization formulated by Matthias Schleiden (1838) and Theodore Schwann (1839) in their paper “Microscopic investigations on the similarities of structure and growth in animals and plants.” - Key Points: - All living things are composed of cells, the smallest living entities. - Cells are the basic unit of structure, function, and organization. - Modification: - Rudolf Virchow (1855) explained that new cells arise from pre-existing cells, finalizing the cell theory. - Modern Cell Theory: - All organisms are composed of one or more cells. - The cell is the basic unit of structure, function, and organization. - All cells arise from pre-existing living cells. - Exception: - Viruses do not conform to cell theory. - Types of Cells - Prokaryotic Cell: - Definition: - Cells without a nuclear membrane; nuclear material (nucleoid) is poorly defined, containing only nucleic acids. - Characteristics: - Smaller (1–10 µm), less complex. - Essentially infoldings of the cell membrane. - Lack most cytoplasmic organelles found in eukaryotic cells. - Examples: - Bacteria (e.g., E. coli, Clostridium), blue-green algae. - Eukaryotic Cell: - Definition: - Cells with a well-organized nucleus enclosed by a nuclear membrane. - Characteristics: - Larger (5–100 µm), more complex. - Nucleus separated from cytoplasm by a double-layered membrane, directing life processes. - Examples: - Protists, fungi, plants, animals. - Note: - Plant and animal eukaryotic cells differ (e.g., plant cells have a cell wall). - Comparison of Prokaryotic and Eukaryotic Cells: -
ComponentsProkaryotic CellEukaryotic Cell
Cell SizeGenerally smaller (1–10 µm)Generally larger (5–100 µm)
Nuclear RegionNot well-defined, no nuclear membrane (nucleoid)Well-defined, surrounded by nuclear membrane
Genetic MaterialCircular DNA, free in cytoplasmLinear DNA with proteins in nucleus
Cell OrganellesLack ER, mitochondria, Golgi apparatus, centrosomesContain ER, mitochondria, Golgi apparatus, lysosomes, centrosomes
VacuolesSap vacuoles absent; gas vacuoles may be presentSap vacuoles common
Ribosomes70S (30S + 50S)80S (40S + 60S)
Endocytosis/ExocytosisDo not occurOccur in many protists and animal cells
Cell CycleShort (20–60 minutes)Long (12–24 hours)
NucleolusAbsentOne or more present
- Eukaryotic Cell Subcategories (Animal vs. Plant Cells): - -
