- Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (AIDS): - Caused by Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV), transmitted via body fluids (e.g., blood, semen), not exclusively an STD. - Damages immune system, causes weight loss, nausea, headache, rashes, pharyngitis. - Transmission modes: Sexual intercourse, blood transfusion, shared syringes/needles. - Human Digestive System - - Definition: - System responsible for digestion, breaking down biomacromolecules in food into simpler forms for utilization through mechanical and biochemical processes. - Alimentary Canal: - Description: - Long tube (8–10 meters) from mouth to anus, facilitating food uptake, digestion, and elimination of indigestible remains. - Parts: - Mouth: - Transverse slit with soft, movable lips (skin externally, mucous membrane internally). - Vestibule: - Narrow space between lips/cheeks (externally) and gums/teeth (internally), lined with mucous glands. - Buccal Cavity: - Contains teeth, tongue, salivary glands; palate forms the upper portion. - Teeth: - Hard structures in upper/lower jaws, embedded in sockets. - Parts: Crown (upper), neck (middle), root (lower). - Types Based on Attachment: - Acrodont: - Attached to bone crest, e.g., snake. - Pleurodont: - Attached to median bone side, e.g., lizard. - Thecodont: - Attached to bony socket, e.g., alligator, humans. - Types Based on Appearance: - Homodont: - All teeth same type, e.g., fish. - Heterodont: - Different types of teeth, e.g., humans. - Types Based on Replacement: - Monophyodont: - Appear once, e.g., human 3rd molar, premolars. - Diphyodont: - Appear twice, e.g., human incisors, canines, 1st/2nd molars. - Polyphyodont: - Appear multiple times, e.g., most lower vertebrates. - Human Teeth: - - Total: - 32 (16 per jaw, 8 per side: 2 incisors, 1 canine, 2 premolars, 3 molars). - Functions: - Incisors: - Biting/cutting (flat, sharp). - Canines: - Tearing/cracking (pointed). - Premolars/Molars: - Crushing/grinding/chewing. - Dental Formula: - \( \frac{ICPmM}{ICPmM} = \frac{2123}{2123} \times 2 = 32 \). - Structure: - Enamel: - Outermost, hardest substance in body (crown). - Cement Layer: - Outermost in neck/root. - Pulp Cavity: - Central, contains pulp (cells, blood/lymph vessels, nerves). - Dentine: - Hard, ivory-like, surrounds pulp cavity. - Tongue: - - Muscular organ, attached to buccal cavity floor via frenulum linguae. - Contains taste buds (sweet, sour, salt, bitter zones). - Functions: - Tasting, swallowing, speech, mastication (mixing saliva). - Everyday Science: - Women have more taste buds than men. - Blue whale has largest tongue (~2.7 metric tons). - Dogs/cats use tongue for cleaning. - 85% of people can curl tongue into a tube. - Capsaicin in chilies irritates heat receptors, causing hot sensation; parrots lack these receptors. - Pharynx: - Small (12 cm) vertical canal, common passage for air/food, communicates with esophagus and trachea. - Epiglottis (cartilaginous flap) prevents food entry into trachea during swallowing. - Oesophagus: - Thin, muscular, conducting tube (23–27 cm), opens into stomach via gullet. - Mucous glands secrete fluid for food movement; gastro-oesophageal sphincter regulates entry to stomach. - Stomach: - J-shaped, widest, most dilated organ; churns food, mixes with gastric juice (pepsin, rennin, HCl). - Mucous lining protects from acidic environment (pH 1.5–2.5). - Parts: - Cardiac: - Upper, receives esophagus. - Fundic: - Middle. - Pyloric: - Lower, opens to duodenum via pyloric sphincter. - Functions: - Liquefies food into chyme, acts as short-term reservoir. - Everyday Science: - Cannot digest cellulose (e.g., grasses, leaves) due to lack of enzymes. - Holds ~1.5 L material. - Lining replaced every 36 hours. - Digests goat milk faster than cow milk. - Intestine: - Small Intestine: - Longest part (~6 m), divided into: - Duodenum: - U-shaped, ~25 cm, widest. - Jejunum: - ~2.5 m, 4 cm diameter. - Ileum: - Longest, ~3.5 cm diameter, thinner walls, opens to colon. - Functions: - Completes digestion of proteins, carbohydrates, nucleic acids, fats; absorbs nutrients into blood/lymph. - Features: - Villi: - Mucosal projections (~1 mm), covered with columnar epithelial cells. - Microvilli: - Microscopic projections on villi cells, give brush border, increase absorptive surface area. - Large Intestine: - Shorter (~1.5 m), wider diameter. - Parts: - Caecum: - Pouch (~6 cm), with vermiform appendix (~8 cm, coiled). - Colon: - Ascending, transverse, descending parts. - Rectum: - ~20 cm, opens to anus. - Functions: - Absorbs water/nutrients, eliminates solid waste. - Digestive Glands: - Salivary Glands: - Three pairs: - Parotid, sublingual, submandibular. - Secrete saliva (pH 6.8, slightly acidic), containing water, electrolytes (Na⁺, K⁺, Cl⁻, HCO₃⁻), mucus, salivary amylase (ptyalin), lysozyme (antibacterial). - Everyday Science: - Produces ~1.5 L saliva daily; triggered by food smell. - Gastric Glands: - Microscopic, tubular glands in stomach epithelium. - Cell Types: - Chief/Peptic Cells: - Secrete inactive proenzymes (pepsinogen, prorennin). - Oxyntic Cells: - Secrete HCl. - Mucous/Goblet Cells: - Secrete alkaline mucus. - Gastric Juice: - pH 1.5–2.5, contains pepsinogen, prorennin, gastric lipase, gastric amylase, mucus, HCl (~2–3 L/day). - Liver: - Largest gland (males: - 1.4–1.8 kg, females: 1.2–1.5 kg), in upper right abdominal cavity below diaphragm. - Structure: - Hepatic lobules (functional units) with hepatic cells, covered by Glisson’s capsule; contains fat storage cells. - Functions: - Bile production (~500–1000 mL/day, pH 8.6 hepatic, 7.6 gall bladder). - Glycogenesis: - Glucose to glycogen (insulin-driven). - Glycogenolysis: - Glycogen to glucose (insulin-driven). - Deamination: - Removes NH₂ from amino acids, forms ammonia → urea. - Excretion: - Bile pigments/waste excreted via feces. - Haemopoiesis: - Blood corpuscle formation. - Heparin secretion: - Anticoagulant. - Enzyme secretion: - For fat, protein, carbohydrate metabolism. - Vitamin-A synthesis: - From β-carotene. - Storage: - Glycogen, fats, vitamins (C, D), bile, blood, water, iron, copper, potassium. - Gall Bladder: - Small, muscular sac below liver; stores/concentrates bile (via cystic/hepatic ducts forming common bile duct). - Function: - Temporary bile storage, concentration; does not produce bile. - Pancreas: - Soft, lobuled, greyish-pink gland (~60 g, 2–5 cm wide, 12–15 cm long), posterior to stomach. - Mixed gland: - Exocrine: - Secretes alkaline pancreatic juice (enzymes, hormones). - Endocrine: - Islets of Langerhans secrete insulin, glucagon into blood. - Intestinal Glands: - In small intestine walls, secrete intestinal juice (succus entericus) with lipolytic, proteolytic, amylolytic enzymes. - Mechanism of Digestion: - Ingestion: - Taking food (solid/liquid) into mouth. - Digestion: - Conversion of complex food into simpler forms via enzymes, begins in mouth, completes in small intestine. - Absorption: - Uptake of digested food by body cells, primarily in small intestine via villi/lacteals (lymph capillaries). - Sites: - Mouth: - Drugs absorbed via mucosa/tongue capillaries. - Stomach: - Water, simple sugars, alcohol. - Small Intestine: - Primary site for nutrients (glucose, fructose, fatty acids, glycerol, amino acids) into blood/lymph. - Large Intestine: - Water, minerals, drugs. - Assimilation: - Distribution of digested food throughout body. - Egestion: - Elimination of undigested food from large intestine (colon, rectum) via anus. - Peristalsis: - Rhythmic muscle contractions move food through digestive tract (esophagus → stomach → small intestine → large intestine → rectum → anus). - Everyday Science: - Stomach noises due to muscle contractions moving food/gastric juices. - Heavy food at night slows digestion, diverts blood from brain, causing poor sleep and health issues. - Digestive Disorders: -
DisorderConditionCausesSymptoms
JaundiceIncreased bile pigment accumulation, excreted via bodyLiver malfunctionYellow skin/eyes
DiarrheaFrequent, liquid bowel movementsStomach lining irritation (virus/bacteria)Cramps, headache, appetite loss, nausea, watery stools
VomitingForceful ejection of stomach contentsInfection, allergy, overeating, food poisoningSweating, nausea
ConstipationDifficult/irregular defecation, prolonged fecal retentionDisrupted diet, low water/fiber, high dairy intakeCramps, pain, vomiting, swelling, difficult bowel movement
- Human Respiratory System - - Overview: - Respiration is the process of releasing energy from food molecules through oxidation, producing ATP (energy currency), CO₂, and water as byproducts. - Involves five steps: 1. Intake of oxygen from the environment. 2. Stepwise oxidation of food using oxygen. 3. Elimination of CO₂ (byproduct). 4. Release of energy during oxidation. 5. Storage of energy as ATP. - Types of Respiration: - Aerobic Respiration: - Involves molecular oxygen intake and CO₂ release; performed by aerobes. - Types: - Direct Respiration: - Exchange of O₂ and CO₂ without respiratory organs or blood, e.g., aerobic bacteria, protists, plants, sponges, coelenterates, flatworms, roundworms, most arthropods. - Indirect Respiration: - Uses specialized organs (skin, buccopharyngeal cells, gills, lungs, blood), e.g., crustaceans, amphibians, annelids, molluscs, reptiles, birds, mammals. - Phases of Indirect Aerobic Respiration: - Breathing: - Physical process of air movement in/out of the body (not synonymous with respiration). - External Respiration: - O₂ intake by blood from air/water in respiratory organs; CO₂ removal. - Internal Respiration (Cellular Respiration): - O₂ uptake by tissue cells from blood via tissue fluid. - Food oxidation in cells, producing energy, water, CO₂. - Energy storage as ATP phosphate bonds. - CO₂ release into blood via tissue fluid. - Anaerobic Respiration (Fermentation): - Energy production without oxygen; performed by anaerobes. - Examples: - Yeast: - Glucose → Ethanol + CO₂ + Energy (C₆H₁₂O₆ → 2C₂H₅OH + 2CO₂). - Bacteria: - Glucose → Lactic acid + Energy (C₆H₁₂O₆ → 2CH₃CHOHCOOH). - Flow Chart of Glucose Oxidation: - Glucose → Glycolysis (in cytoplasm) → Pyruvate + ATP - With O₂ (mitochondria): CO₂ + H₂O + Energy. - Without O₂ (human muscle): Lactic acid. - Without O₂ (yeast): Ethanol + CO₂. - Human Respiratory System Organs: - Nostrils: - Paired nose openings leading to nasal cavities; entry point for air. - Nasal Cavities: - Two cavities separated by nasal septum (cartilaginous partition). - Pharynx: - Comprises nasopharynx, oropharynx, laryngopharynx; common passage for air and food. - Larynx (Voice Box): - Upper trachea part, allows air passage; larger in men post-puberty (Adam’s apple). - Epiglottis prevents food entry into trachea. - Trachea (Wind Pipe): - Long tube with incomplete hyaline cartilage rings, bifurcates into primary bronchi. - Bronchi branch into bronchioles, terminating in alveoli (grape-like sacs surrounded by capillaries). - Lungs: - Paired, soft, spongy, elastic, pinkish organs in thoracic cavity, enclosed by pleurae (parietal and visceral, with pleural fluid). - Parts: - Apex (uppermost), base (inferior), cardiac notch (left lung). - Thoracic cavity closed by diaphragm. - Everyday Science: - One lung sufficient for survival, but limits physical ability. - Nacrosis: - Depression of respiratory centers in CNS. - Pharyngitis: - Pharynx inflammation (sore throat). - Snoring: - Indicates potential breathing issues during sleep. - Avoid sleeping with covered face to prevent O₂ blockage/CO₂ buildup, causing suffocation/health issues. - Movement of Air: - Path: - Nostrils → Pharynx → Larynx → Trachea → Bronchi → Bronchioles → Alveoli → Blood capillaries → Tissues. - Mechanism of Respiration: - Breathing: - Inspiration (O₂ intake) and expiration (CO₂ release). - Occurs 14–18 times/min at rest (higher in children). - Mammals use negative pressure breathing, allowing simultaneous eating/breathing. - Thoracic breathing predominant in females. - Gaseous Exchange: - O₂/CO₂ exchange between alveoli (primary site) and blood due to partial pressure differences. - Transport of Gases: - Oxygen: - 3% dissolved, 97% as oxyhaemoglobin to tissues. - Carbon Dioxide: - 7% dissolved, 70% as bicarbonate ions, 23% as carbaminohaemoglobin to lungs. - Respiratory Quotient (RQ): - Ratio of CO₂ released to O₂ absorbed: \( RQ = \frac{\text{Volume of CO}_2 \text{ formed}}{\text{Volume of O}_2 \text{ utilized}} \). - Values: - Carbohydrates: - 1. - Proteins: - 0.90. - Fats: - 0.70. - Anaerobic respiration: - ∞. - Cellular Respiration: - Releases chemical energy from food, captured as ATP (oxidative phosphorylation). - Fuels: - Carbohydrates, fats, proteins (glucose most common). - Stages: 1. Glycolysis: - Glucose (6C) → Pyruvate (3C) in cytosol. 2. Transition Reaction: - Pyruvate decarboxylated, forms acetyl CoA (links glycolysis to Kreb’s cycle). 3. Kreb’s Cycle: - Acetyl CoA oxidation in mitochondria (aerobic). 4. Electron Transport Chain (ETC): - Electrons passed to final acceptor via coenzymes/cytochromes in mitochondria/chloroplasts. - Disorders of Respiratory System: -
DisorderCausesSymptomsPreventionsCure
AsthmaAllergens trigger histamine release, causing bronchi/bronchiole inflammationCoughing, sneezing, mucus clogging bronchi/bronchiolesAvoid allergensAntibiotics, bronchodilators, inhalers
Occupational Respiratory Disorders (e.g., silicosis, asbestosis)Chronic exposure to silica, asbestos; failed body defenseFibrosis of upper lung, inflammationMinimize dust exposure, use protective gear, regular checkupsNo permanent cure; symptomatic treatment (bronchodilators, antibiotics)
PneumoniaBacteria (Streptococcus pneumoniae), fungi, virusesInflamed alveoli filled with fluid/dead WBCsPathogen protectionAntibiotics for bacterial infection
EmphysemaCigarette smoking, toxic smoke inhalation damaging alveolar wallsReduced gas exchange area, lung inflation, difficult exhalationAvoid smoking, pollutantsNo permanent cure; symptomatic treatment (bronchodilators, antibiotics, oxygen therapy)
- Additional Conditions: - Hypoxia: - Oxygen shortage in tissues. - Apnea: - Prolonged respiration pause during inspiration. - Atelectasis: - Lung collapse, absence of air in part/whole lung. - Hay Fever: - Acute inflammation of upper respiratory mucous membrane. - Anoxia: - Absence of oxygen in inspired gases, arterial blood, or tissues. - Human Circulatory System - Definition: - System responsible for circulating nutrients, disposing of waste, and excreting waste materials in the body. - Types of Circulatory Systems: - Open Circulatory System: - Blood pumped by heart into aorta, branches into arteries, opens into haemocoel (spaces), e.g., most arthropods, some cephalopods. - Closed Circulatory System: - Blood remains in vessels, not in direct contact with tissues; entry/exit via vessel walls, e.g., annelids, chordates (including humans). - Advantage: - Supplies blood to deepest tissues efficiently. - Human Blood Vascular System: - Components: - Blood, blood vessels, heart. - Blood: - Fluid connective tissue, 30–32% of extracellular fluid, ~5–5.5 L in adults. - Red due to haemoglobin in red blood cells (RBCs). - Components: - Plasma (55%): - Straw-colored, viscous, slightly alkaline (pH ~7.4), 90–92% water, 6–8% solutes. - Solutes: - Ions (Na⁺, Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺, HCO₃⁻), glucose, plasma proteins (fibrinogen, globulins, albumins), amino acids, hormones, cholesterol, lipids, urea, organic acids. - Plasma Proteins: - Provide viscosity; deficiency causes swelling (edema) in legs/hands. - Minerals/Inorganic Salts (Electrolytes): - Na⁺, Cl⁻ (principal), bicarbonate, phosphate; maintained by kidneys (homeostasis). - Anticoagulant: - Heparin (heteropolysaccharide, produced in liver) prevents clotting in uninjured vessels by inhibiting prothrombin → thrombin. - Serum: - Plasma without clotting factors. - Functions: - Transports and distributes heat. - Provides immunity. - Maintains blood pH. - Prevents blood loss (fibrinogen for clotting). - Globulins aid defense; albumins maintain osmotic balance. - Blood Corpuscles (45%): - Erythrocytes (RBCs): - Biconcave, anucleate, 4.5–5 million/mm³, 7–8 µm diameter. - Contain haemoglobin; formed via erythropoiesis (liver/spleen pre-birth, bone marrow post-birth). - Lifespan: - ~120 days. - Function: - Oxygen transport. - Leucocytes (WBCs): - Colorless, nucleated, motile, 6,000–8,000/mm³, 12–20 µm. - Formed via leucopoiesis (bone marrow, lymph nodes, spleen, thymus, tonsils, Peyer’s patches). - Lifespan: - Hours–days (granulocytes), months (agranulocytes). - Types: - Granulocytes (60–65%): - Neutrophils: - Multilobed nucleus, phagocytic, correspond to macrophages. - Eosinophils: - Bilobed nucleus, non-phagocytic, involved in allergies/hypersensitivity. - Basophils: - Three-lobed nucleus, non-phagocytic, contain heparin, histamine, serotonin. - Agranulocytes: - Lymphocytes (B-cells, T-cells): - Large rounded nucleus, non-phagocytic, produce antibodies. - Monocytes: - Bean-shaped nucleus, phagocytic, engulf bacteria/debris. - Thrombocytes (Platelets): - Anucleate, disc-shaped, 150,000–350,000/mm³, 2–3 µm. - Formed via thrombopoiesis from megakaryocytes in bone marrow. - Lifespan: - 8–10 days. - Function: - Blood clotting. - Blood Clotting: - Prevents excessive blood loss post-injury. - Process: - Thromboplastin → prothrombinase (inactivates heparin) → prothrombin (inactive, liver-produced, vitamin K-dependent) → thrombin → fibrinogen → fibrin monomer → clot with blood cells. - Everyday Science: - Blood stored in heparinized vials during donation to prevent clotting. - Functions of Blood: - Maintains pH, water, ionic balance. - Aids wound healing. - Transports hormones from endocrine glands. - Facilitates coagulation. - Transports wastes to kidneys. - Maintains body temperature. - Fights infections (immunity). - Transports respiratory gases (O₂, CO₂). - Blood Groups: - Based on glycoprotein antigens on RBC plasma membrane. - ABO Blood Grouping: - Antigens A, B, or absent (O) on RBCs; plasma contains anti-A, anti-B antibodies. - Rh Grouping: - Rh antigen (Rhesus monkey-like) on ~80% of humans (Rh⁺); absent in Rh⁻. - Rh Incompatibility: - Rh⁻ mother with Rh⁺ fetus may develop anti-Rh antibodies, risking erythroblastosis fetalis (fetal RBC destruction, anemia, jaundice) in subsequent pregnancies. Prevented by anti-Rh antibody administration post-first delivery. - Everyday Science: - Women: - ~4.5 L blood; men: ~5.6 L. - O Rh⁻: - Universal donors; AB Rh⁺: Universal receivers. - Rh⁺ blood cannot be given to Rh⁻ individuals. - Heart: - Muscular, mesodermal organ in thoracic cavity, ~12 cm, 280–340 g (males), 230–280 g (females). - Surrounded by pericardium (two-layered, with pericardial fluid). - Types (Vertebrates): - 2-chambered (1 atrium, 1 ventricle): - Single circulation, deoxygenated blood, e.g., fish. - 3-chambered (2 atria, 1 ventricle): - Incomplete double circulation, mixed blood, e.g., amphibians, reptiles (except crocodiles). - 4-chambered (2 atria, 2 ventricles): - Double circulation, no mixing, e.g., birds, mammals, crocodiles. - Human Heart Structure: - - Four chambers: - Atria (Auricles): - Left/right, separated by inter-auricular septum; SA node (pacemaker) in right atrium. - Ventricles: - Left/right, separated by inter-ventricular septum; thicker walls for pumping blood. - Parts and Functions: - Left Atrium: - Receives oxygenated blood from lungs (pulmonary veins). - Left Ventricle: - Pumps oxygenated blood to body (aorta). - Right Atrium: - Receives deoxygenated blood from body (vena cava). - Right Ventricle: - Pumps deoxygenated blood to lungs (pulmonary artery). - Valves: - Atrioventricular (AV): - Mitral (Bicuspid): - Left atrium to left ventricle. - Tricuspid: - Right atrium to right ventricle. - Prevent backflow during systole. - Semilunar (SL): - In arteries leaving heart (aortic, pulmonary), prevent backflow. - Aorta: - Carries oxygenated blood. - Septum: - Prevents mixing of oxygenated/deoxygenated blood. - Pulmonary Artery: - Carries deoxygenated blood to lungs. - Pulmonary Vein: - Carries oxygenated blood to heart. - Vena Cava (Superior/Inferior): - Returns deoxygenated blood from head/arms (superior) or lower body (inferior). - Working Mechanism: - Systole (contraction) and diastole (relaxation) of atria/ventricles alternate, forming a heartbeat (~72 beats/min at rest, up to 180 for physical workers). - SA node initiates contractions, spreads to atria, reaches AV node (pacesetter), stimulates ventricles via Bundle of His and Purkinje fibers. - Heart Sounds: - Lub: - AV valves close, SL valves open (ventricular systole start). - Dub: - SL valves close, AV valves open (ventricular systole end). - Electrocardiograph (ECG): - Graphical record of cardiac muscle electrical activity; deviations indicate abnormal heart function. - - Pumping Action: - Double Circulation: - Pulmonary: - Deoxygenated blood from right ventricle to lungs, oxygenated blood returns to left atrium. - Systemic: - Oxygenated blood from left ventricle to body, deoxygenated blood returns to right atrium. - Advantage: - Efficient oxygen delivery to lungs and body. - Everyday Science: - Heart valves open/close ~100,000 times daily. - Women’s heart rate: - ~78 beats/min; men’s: ~70 beats/min. - Blood travel times: - 6s (heart-lungs-heart), 8s (heart-brain-heart), 16s (heart-toes-heart). - Cocaine disrupts heart electrical activity, causes artery spasms, risks heart attack/stroke. - Left atrium pumps 75% blood to left ventricle effortlessly, allowing survival with defective left atrium/mitral valve. - Blood Vessels: - Network of tubes transporting blood; layers (except capillaries): tunica externa, media, interna. - Types: - Arteries: - Elastic, carry blood from heart (oxygenated, except pulmonary artery). - Veins: - Elastic, carry blood to heart (deoxygenated, except pulmonary veins). - Capillaries: - Thin-walled, connect arteries to veins, enable exchange of nutrients, wastes, O₂, CO₂. - Blood Pressure: - Force of blood against vessel walls due to left ventricle contraction, measured in mmHg via sphygmomanometer. - Systolic: - During ventricle contraction (~120 mmHg). - Diastolic: - During heart rest (~80 mmHg). - Average: - 120/80 mmHg; highest in arteries, lowest in veins. - Cardiac Output: - Blood volume pumped per minute: Stroke Volume (SV, ~70 mL) × Heart Rate (HR, ~75) = ~5,250 mL/min (~total blood volume). - Human Lymphatic System: - Components: - Lymph, lymph capillaries, lymphatic vessels, lymph nodes, lymphatic ducts. - Lymph: - Colorless fluid, similar to tissue fluid and blood plasma; contains low nutrients/O₂, high CO₂/wastes, amoeboid WBCs. - Formation: - Interstitial fluid (ECF) from blood plasma enters lymph vessels, becoming lymph. - Functions: - Transports nutrients, hormones. - Renews ECF. - Absorbs fat via lacteals in intestinal villi. - Keeps tissue cells moist. - Supports lymphocyte (B/T-cells) maturation in lymph nodes. - Lymphatic Capillaries: - Thin-walled, single endothelial layer; unite to form larger lymphatic vessels with valves, draining into thoracic/right lymphatic ducts. - Lymphatic Nodes: - Small, oval/bean-shaped structures along lymphatic vessels (thoracic mediastinum, posterior abdominal wall, pelvis, neck, limb ends). - Produce B/T-lymphocytes; contain phagocytic WBCs/macrophages to filter lymph. - Disorders of Circulatory System: -
DisorderDescription
Hypertension (High Blood Pressure)Blood pressure >140/90 mmHg; harms heart, brain, kidneys, eyes.
ArteriosclerosisHardening/loss of artery elasticity; walls may rupture, clot in coronary artery causing heart attack.
Coronary Artery Disease (CAD, Atherosclerosis)Plaque accumulation in coronary artery walls, reducing oxygen/nutrient supply to myocardium.
Heart AttackSudden decrease in coronary blood supply, causing cardiac muscle death; symptoms: severe chest pain, breathlessness, restlessness, nausea, vomiting.
HypotensionLow arterial blood pressure.
Cardiac ArrestHeart stops beating.
- Human Excretory System - - Definition: - Excretion: - Removal of metabolic waste products (solid, liquid, gas) from the body. - Major Excretory Products: - Carbon dioxide, excess water, nitrogenous compounds (ammonia, urea, uric acid). - Modes of Excretion: - Ammonotelism: - Excrete ammonia (NH₃), highly toxic, water-soluble; e.g., aquatic animals (Amoeba, Paramecium, Sycon, Hydra, prawns). - Ureotelism: - Excrete urea (NH₂CONH₂), less toxic; e.g., humans, whales, seals, kangaroos, frogs, toads. - Uricotelism: - Excrete uric acid, conserves water in dry conditions; e.g., snakes, lizards, birds, insects, snails. - Excretory Organs in Various Organisms: -
OrganismExcretory Organ
Amoeba, Coelenterates (Hydra)General body surface, contractile vacuole (osmoregulation)
CockroachesMalpighian tubules
SpidersCoxal glands
EarthwormsNephridia
FlatwormsFlame cells or solenocytes
PrawnsGreen glands
Humans (Mammals)Kidneys
- Osmoregulation: - Balancing osmotic pressure between extracellular (blood) and intracellular fluids, maintaining saltwater balance. - Human Excretory System: - Components: - Kidneys (pair) - Ureters (pair) - Urinary bladder - Urethra - Other Excretory Organs: - Skin, lungs, liver. - Kidneys: - - Main excretory organs, reddish-brown, bean-shaped, 10–12 cm long, 5–7 cm wide, 2–3 cm thick, 120–170 g. - Located between last thoracic and 3rd lumbar vertebrae, on either side of vertebral column. - Structure: - Enclosed in fibrous capsule; outer convex, inner concave with hilum (entry/exit for renal artery/vein). - Zones: - Outer cortex (dark, granular), inner medulla (lighter, with medullary pyramids projecting into calyces). - Renal pelvis: - Bag-shaped cavity, directs into ureter. - Functional Unit: - Nephrons (~1 million/kidney) and collecting ducts. - Nephron: - - Controls blood chemical composition; consists of: - Malpighian Body (Renal Corpuscle): - Glomerulus: - Capillary network from afferent (in) and efferent (out) arterioles. - Bowman’s Capsule: - Double-walled, cup-like, surrounds glomerulus; filters large solutes from blood, delivers small solutes to renal tubule. - Renal Tubules: - Proximal Convoluted Tubule (PCT) - Henle’s Loop: - Hairpin structure between PCT and DCT. - Distal Convoluted Tubule (DCT): - Opens into collecting duct. - Location: - Malpighian body, PCT, DCT in cortex; Henle’s loop in medulla. - Types: - Cortical Nephrons: - Short Henle’s loop, dips slightly into medulla. - Juxtamedullary Nephrons: - Long Henle’s loop, deep into medulla. - Functions: - Removes excess water, nitrogenous wastes (urea, uric acid) as urine. - Maintains blood plasma concentration (osmoregulation). - Regulates blood pH. - Ureters: - Long, muscular tubes from renal pelvis (at hilum) to urinary bladder. - Urinary Bladder: - Thin-walled, pear-shaped, transparent sac in pelvic cavity; stores urine temporarily. - Urethra: - Membranous tube for urine excretion; controlled by urethral sphincters (closed except during voiding). - Mechanism of Urine Formation (Uropoiesis): - Begins in liver (urea formation via ornithine cycle), completed in kidneys via three stages: - Glomerular Filtration: - High blood pressure in glomerular capillaries filters blood (ultrafiltration), producing protein-free, cell-free filtrate (~180 L/day). - Glomerular Filtration Rate (GFR): ~125 mL/min in healthy adults. - Selective Reabsorption: - Reabsorbs selective molecules (e.g., glucose, ions) from filtrate via diffusion, active/passive transport. - ~1–1.8 L urine excreted daily; rest reabsorbed. - Tubular Secretion: - Transfers remaining substances (e.g., H⁺, NH₄⁺) from blood to filtrate, maintaining ionic balance and blood pH (6–8). - Everyday Science: - Kidneys filter ~1,100–1,200 mL blood/min (~1/5 of cardiac output). - Ultrafiltrate resembles protein-free, cell-free plasma. - H⁺, NH₄⁺ removal in PCT/DCT maintains blood pH; deviations are dangerous. - Urine: - Fluid and dissolved waste excreted by kidneys via nephrons. - Physical Properties: - Transparent, yellowish (due to urochrome from haemoglobin breakdown). - Volume depends on fluid intake, physical activity, food type, environmental temperature. - Chemical Composition: - Water: - ~95%. - Organic Substances (~2.5%): - Uric acid, creatinine, urea, hippuric acid, phenolic substances, traces of glucose, vitamin C. - Inorganic Substances (~2.5%): - Ammonia, mineral salts (chlorides, sulphates, phosphates of Na, Ca, K). - Other: - Drugs, pigments. - pH: - 5–8 (diet-dependent: high protein/fruits increase acidity; vegetables increase alkalinity). - Diuretics (e.g., tea, coffee, alcohol) increase urine output. - Artificial Kidney (Haemodialysis): - Device for removing nitrogenous wastes from blood in kidney failure (caused by infections, injury, reduced blood flow). - Unlike natural kidneys, no reabsorption; processes ~180 L filtrate daily, excreting ~1–1.8 L. - Functions of Other Excretory Organs: - Liver: - Excretes bile (contains haem pigments), cholesterol. - Skin: - Excretes water, urea, salts via sweat glands. - Lungs: - Excrete carbon dioxide. - Disorders of Excretory System: -
DisorderDescription
Kidney Failure (Renal Failure)Partial/total inability of kidneys to perform excretory and saltwater regulation functions.
Renal CalculiFormation of kidney stones (crystallized salts: calcium, magnesium, phosphates, oxalates).
GlomerulonephritisInflammation of glomeruli.
UremiaExcessive urea in blood, toxic at high concentrations, may lead to kidney failure.
- Human Skeletal System - - Definition: - Framework of bones and cartilages that protects organs and enables movement; includes bones and joints (specialized connective tissues). - Types of Skeletal System: - Exoskeleton: - External skeleton, originates from ectoderm/mesoderm, e.g., nails, hairs. - Non-living materials, muscles attached to inner surface, may restrict growth, periodically moulted. - Endoskeleton: - Internal skeleton, originates from mesoderm, e.g., bones, cartilages. - Living tissues, muscles attached to outer surface, does not restrict growth, never moulted. - Functions of Skeletal System: - Protection: - Shields internal organs (e.g., brain by cranial bones, spinal cord by vertebrae, heart/lungs by ribcage). - Support: - Provides structure for softer body parts. - Movement: - Muscles attached to bones enable movement via contraction. - Mineral Reservation: - Stores calcium, phosphorus for body use. - Haematopoiesis: - Bone marrow produces red/white blood cells, platelets. - Breathing and Hearing: - Larynx, sternum, ribs, trachea cartilages aid breathing; ear ossicles transmit sound. - Body Form: - Provides shape and size. - Body Balance: - Skeletal distribution maintains balance. - Components: - Bones: - Adult human: - 206 bones (from 270 at birth due to fusion). - Living tissues with blood vessels, composed of proteins, minerals (calcium salts for hardness), vitamins. - Types: - Long Bones: - Elongated shaft (diaphysis), two expanded ends (epiphyses), medullary cavity; e.g., femur, ulna. - Short Bones: - Broad, variable shape; e.g., carpals, tarsals. - Flat Bones: - Shallow plates, form body cavity boundaries; e.g., scapula, ribs, sternum. - Irregular Bones: - Complex shapes; e.g., hip bone, vertebrae, skull base bones. - Pneumatic Bones: - Contain air spaces, lightweight; e.g., sphenoid, ethmoid. - Sesamoid Bones: - Nodule-like, embedded in tendons/joint capsules, ossify post-birth; e.g., patella. - Everyday Science: - Femur: - Longest, strongest, hollow bone. - Stapes (ear): - Smallest bone, fully grown at birth. - Lumbar vertebrae: - Largest, strongest in vertebral column. - Female sacrum: - Shorter, wider than male. - Fingernails grow ~4x faster than toenails. - Cartilages: - Connective tissues with pliable matrix (chondroitin salts), semi-solid, transparent, elastic glycoprotein. - Human Skeletal System Structure: - 206 bones divided into: - Axial Skeleton (80 bones): - Skull: - Cranium: - Occipital (1), parietal (2), frontal (1), temporal (2), sphenoid (1), ethmoid (1). - Facial: - Nasal (2), vomer (1), turbinal (2), lacrimal (2), zygomatic (2), palatine (2), maxilla (2), mandible (1). - Ear Ossicles: - Malleus (2), incus (2), stapes (2). - Hyoid: - Hyoid body (1). - Vertebral Column: - Cervical vertebrae (7), thoracic vertebrae (12), lumbar vertebrae (5), sacrum (1, fused from 5), coccyx (1, fused from 4). - Thorax: - Sternum (1), ribs (24). - Appendicular Skeleton (126 bones): - Pectoral Girdle: - Scapula (2), clavicle (2). - Pelvic Girdle: - Os innominatum (2). - Forelimbs: - Upper arm: - Humerus (2). - Forearm: - Radius, ulna (4). - Wrist: - Carpals (16). - Palm: - Metacarpals (10). - Fingers: - Phalanges (28). - Hindlimbs: - Thigh: - Femur (2). - Shank: - Tibia, fibula (4). - Knee: - Patella (2). - Ankle: - Tarsals (14). - Sole: - Metatarsals (10). - Fingers: - Phalanges (28). - Everyday Science: - Hydroskeleton: - Fluid-based support in Hydra. - Joints: - Structural arrangements joining bone to bone or bone to cartilage. - Types (by Mobility): - Fibrous (Fixed/Immovable): - No movement, bones held by fibrous tissue (sutures); e.g., skull sutures, teeth to maxilla/mandible. - Cartilaginous (Slightly Movable): - Limited movement due to fibrous cartilage pad compression; e.g., pubic symphysis, intervertebral discs. - Synovial (Freely Movable): - Contain synovial cavity, articular cartilage, synovial membrane, sometimes ligaments; freely movable in specific directions. - Categories: - Hinge (Ginglymus): - Monaxial, single-plane movement; e.g., elbow, ankle, interphalangeal joints. - Pivot: - Rotational, monaxial, one bone rotates within a ring of another bone/ligament; e.g., head rotation, radius-ulna proximal ends. - Gliding: - Flat surfaces, side-to-side/back-and-forth movement; e.g., carpal, tarsal, sternum-clavicle, scapula-clavicle joints. - Ball and Socket (Sphenoid): - Triaxial, ball-like surface in cup-like cavity; e.g., shoulder, hip. - Saddle (Sellaris): - Biaxial, saddle-shaped surface; e.g., carpal-metacarpal of thumb. - Ellipsoidal (Condyloid): - Biaxial, oval condyle in elliptical cavity; e.g., wrist. - Functions: - Provide flexibility. - Allow growth of connected structures. - Essential for movement. - Common Injuries: - Dislocation: - Bones forced out of joint, often with sprains, inflammation, immobilization. - Cartilage Tears: - Due to joint twisting or pressure. - Sprain: - Partially/completely torn ligament. - Disorders of Skeletal System: -
DisorderDescription
Arthritis (Aching Joints)Inflammation of joints, common in old age due to reduced synovial fluid, causing bone weakening.
Osteomalacia/RicketsChildhood disease with insufficient calcium/phosphorus in bones.
BursitisInflammation of joint bursae due to injury or prolonged pressure.
Paget’s DiseaseAbnormal bone reabsorption by abnormal osteoclasts, causing irregular thickening/softening and bone deformation.
Muscular DystrophyGirdle muscle deterioration in early age, leading to inability to walk post-12, cardiomyopathy, mental impairment, death by ~20 from cardiac/respiratory failure; common in males.
Myasthenia GravisAutoimmune disease affecting neuromuscular junction, causing fatigue, muscle weakening, paralysis.
TetanyFrequent muscle spasms due to low Ca²⁺ in body fluids.
- Human Nervous System - Definition: - Ectodermal system in animals (absent in plants) for control and coordination of body functions (responding, understanding, memorizing) via neurons; also called neural system. - Works with endocrine system to maintain physiology. - Neurons (Nerve Cells): - - Structural and functional units of neural tissue; longest cells in the body (~100 billion, mostly in brain). - Detect stimuli from receptors, transmit to central nervous system (CNS). - Structure: - Cell Body (Cyton/Soma): - Varies in shape (irregular, spherical, oval, round, star-shaped, pyramidal). - Contains neurofibrils (impulse transmission), Nissl’s granules (protein synthesis, like rough ER with ribosomes), neurotubules (maintain shape). - Function: - Metabolic maintenance, growth. - Neurites: - Dendrites: - Short, tapering, branched; contain neurofibrils, neurotubules, Nissl’s granules; conduct impulses toward cell body (afferent process). - Axon: - Long, uniform thickness, arises from axon hillock; depends on cell body for proteins; ends in synaptic knobs with neurotransmitters; conducts impulses away from cell body (efferent process). - Types: - Myelinated: Enveloped by Schwann cells forming myelin sheath; found in spinal/cranial nerves. - Unmyelinated: Enveloped by Schwann cells without myelin; found in autonomic/somatic motor neurons. - Types of Neurons: - Functional: - Sensory (Afferent): - Conduct impulses from receptors to CNS; dendrite ends modified as receptors. - Interneurons: - Connect sensory/motor neurons; carry stimuli in brain/spinal cord. - Motor: - Conduct impulses from CNS to effectors (muscles/glands). - Structural (by dendrites/axons): - Multipolar: - One axon, multiple dendrites. - Bipolar: - One axon, one dendrite. - Unipolar: - One axon only. - Human Nervous System Components: - Central Nervous System (CNS): - Brain and spinal cord. - Peripheral Nervous System (PNS): - Nerves connecting CNS to receptors/effectors; includes 12 pairs cranial nerves (from brain), 31 pairs spinal nerves (from spinal cord). - Autonomic Nervous System (ANS): - Transmits impulses from CNS to involuntary organs/smooth muscles; discovered by Langley (1921). - Divisions: - Sympathetic: - Accelerates heartbeat, enlarges pupils, supplies blood to muscles, contracts bladder nerves, reduces digestion, aids clotting, increases sweat, eases breathing, promotes liver sugar release, reduces bile. - Parasympathetic: - Slows heartbeat, dilates arteries, lowers blood pressure, speeds peristalsis, stimulates salivary glands, contracts gallbladder, promotes pancreatic secretion, reduces sweat. - CNS Details: - Brain: - Anterior CNS part, in cranial cavity (skull); weighs 1,220–1,400 g. - Covered by meninges: - Piamater (innermost, vascular), arachnoid (thin, web-like), duramater (outermost, tough, adheres to skull). - Cerebrospinal Fluid: - In subarachnoid space (between piamater and arachnoid); cushions CNS, facilitates exchange of nutrients, wastes, gases. - Parts: - Forebrain (Prosencephalon): - Olfactory Lobes: - Paired, club-shaped, covered by cerebral hemispheres; sense of smell. - Cerebrum: - Largest, most complex; two hemispheres connected by corpus callosum (myelinated fibers). Cerebral cortex (outer) has gyri (folds); grey matter (outer), white matter (inner). Contains sensory (receive impulses) and motor (send to effectors) areas. - Diencephalon: - Thalamus: - Grey matter, lateral walls; relays sensory inputs, no hormone production. - Epithalamus: - Thin membrane, posterior segment. - Hypothalamus: - Controls eating/drinking urges, thermoregulation, visceral activities. - Midbrain (Mesencephalon): - Between forebrain (thalamus/hypothalamus) and hindbrain (pons); contains cerebral aqueduct. - With hindbrain, forms brainstem. - Functions: - Relays impulses between cerebrum, cerebellum, pons, medulla; involved in vision. - Hindbrain (Rhombencephalon): - Cerebellum: - Second largest, convoluted surface for more neurons; controls rapid, involuntary muscular activities (e.g., running, typing, talking). - Pons: - Below midbrain, above medulla; relays impulses, regulates breathing. - Medulla Oblongata: - Extends from pons to spinal cord; regulates heart rate, blood pressure, breathing, swallowing, salivation, sneezing, coughing, vomiting. - Spinal Cord (Myelon): - Extends from brain base to second lumbar vertebra, 42–45 cm long, in vertebral column’s neural canal. - Covered by meninges (duramater, arachnoid, piamater). - Divided by posterior median sulcus, anterior median tissues into left/right halves; grey matter (inner), white matter (outer). - Functions: - Conducts stimuli to/from brain, center for spinal reflex actions. - Reflex Arc: - Pathway for reflex action (autonomous response to stimulus via spinal cord, no brain involvement). - Process: - Sensory nerve endings detect stimulus → sensory neurons to spinal cord → interneurons synapse → motor neurons to muscles/glands. - Some reflexes involve brain for interpretation. - Examples: - Stepping on sharp object, touching hot plate, knee jerk, diaphragm movement, coughing, yawning. - Importance: - Controls body activities, fast/accurate response to harmful stimuli, coordinates activities. - Sensory Reception and Processing: - Sensory organs (eyes, ears, nose, tongue, skin) respond to stimuli, send signals to CNS for processing, then to brain centers. - Eye (Photoreceptors): - - Paired, in skull orbits; spherical, ~2.3 cm diameter. - Parts: - Cornea: - Transparent, front membrane; refracts light. - Lens: - Central, forms images. - Iris: - Muscular diaphragm, controls pupil size, determines eye color. - Pupil: - Hole for light entry; adjusts size (large in dim light, small in bright). - Ciliary Muscles: - Hold lens, adjust curvature. - Retina: - Light-sensitive, with rods (~125 million, intensity) and cones (color); signals sent via optic nerves. - Optic Nerves: - Transmit visual info to brain. - Sclera: - Opaque, protective outer layer (white of eye). - Blind Spot: - Optic nerve exit, no rods/cones. - Aqueous Humour: - Between cornea and lens, maintains pressure. - Vitreous Humour: - Between lens and retina, aids focus. - Mechanism of Vision: - Light focuses on retina via cornea/lens, stimulates rods/cones. - Opsin (protein) and retinal (vitamin A aldehyde) dissociate under light, altering opsin structure and membrane permeability. - Potential difference in photoreceptors generates action potential via ganglion and bipolar cells. - Optic nerves transmit to brain’s visual cortex for analysis based on memory/experience. - Everyday Science: - Iris adjusts pupil size for light entry. - Pupil appears black (no light reflection). - Babies lack tears until 6–8 weeks, are colorblind at birth. - Men read fine print better than women. - Shark cornea used in human eye surgery. - Birds have sharper vision, see UV, have more cones, no retinal blood vessels, more rods (e.g., owls for night vision). - Least distance of vision varies with age: 10 years (18 cm), 20 years (25 cm), 40 years (50 cm), 60 years (200+ cm). - Ear: - - Functions: - Hearing, balance. - Structure: - Outer Ear: - Pinna: - Collects sound vibrations. - External Auditory Meatus: - Channels sound to tympanic membrane (eardrum). - Middle Ear: - Contains ossicles (malleus, incus, stapes) in chain; amplifies sound to inner ear. Eustachian tube equalizes pressure with pharynx. - Inner Ear: - Fluid-filled labyrinth (bony and membranous); includes vestibular apparatus (balance/posture), cochlea (hearing). - Mechanism of Hearing: - Pinna directs sound to eardrum, which vibrates. - Vibrations pass through ossicles to oval window, then cochlear fluid, generating waves. - Afferent neurons produce nerve impulses, transmitted via auditory nerves to brain’s auditory cortex for sound recognition. - Nose (Smell): - Nasal cavity lined with mucous membranes containing smell receptors linked to olfactory nerve. - Receptors detect vapor molecules, transmit sensations to brain. - Vomeronasal organ: - Possible pheromone sensitivity, affects reproductive cycle. - Everyday Science: - Dogs’ sense of smell far more sensitive than humans’; colds may temporarily impair smell. - Skin (Touch): - Nerve endings throughout body transmit touch sensations (cold, heat, contact, pain) to brain. - Fingertips, sexual organs have highest nerve ending density; hairs enhance sensitivity as early warning system. - Disorders of Nervous System: - (Not detailed in provided text, but common examples include): - Epilepsy: - Seizures due to abnormal brain electrical activity. - Parkinson’s Disease: - Motor control loss from dopamine deficiency. - Alzheimer’s Disease: - Progressive memory/cognitive decline. - Stroke: - Brain damage from disrupted blood supply. - Multiple Sclerosis: - Autoimmune attack on myelin, impairing nerve signals. - Human Endocrine System - - Definition: - System responsible for chemical coordination via endocrine glands and hormones, regulating body functions. - Works with nervous system to maintain physiology. - Glands: - Organs synthesizing and secreting substances. - Types: - Exocrine Glands: - Have ducts, secretions carried to specific organs for metabolic activities, e.g., salivary glands, liver. - Endocrine Glands: - Ductless, secrete hormones into blood for specific target organs, e.g., thyroid, parathyroid, adrenals. - Hormones: - Non-nutrient chemicals (amino acid derivatives, peptides, steroids) secreted by endocrine glands in trace amounts in response to environmental changes. - Act as intercellular messengers, regulating biological processes. - Discovered by William M. Bayliss and Ernest H. Starling (1903, secretin); term "hormone" coined by Starling (1905). - Characteristics: - Released directly into blood for circulation. - Secreted in small amounts. - Regulate growth, development, behavior, sexual characteristics, reproduction, metabolism. - Maintain body balance (homeostasis). - Human Endocrine System Components: - Endocrine Glands: - Thyroid, parathyroid, adrenal, pituitary, thymus, pineal. - Mixed Glands: - Pancreas (exocrine and endocrine functions). - Other Structures: - Hypothalamus, skin, gastrointestinal mucosa, heart, kidneys, gonads (ovaries/testes), placenta, liver. - Endocrine Glands, Hormones, and Functions: -

- Everyday Science: - Endocrinology: - Study of endocrine glands and hormones. - First hormone discovered: - Secretin (1903); first isolated: Insulin (Banting, McLeod). - Hypothalamus: - Supreme commander of endocrine system. - Pituitary: - Smallest endocrine gland, considered "orchestra" of endocrine system (previously "master gland"). - Thyroid: - Largest endocrine gland. - Disorders of Endocrine System: -
DisorderDescription
DwarfismDeficiency of Growth Hormone (GH) in childhood, causing premature cessation of long bone/body growth.
GigantismExcess GH in childhood, resulting in abnormal height and long bones.
AcromegalyHigh GH post-adolescence (e.g., from acidophilic tumor), causing enlarged jaw/limbs without giant stature.
CretinismThyroid hormone hyposecretion in infants, leading to slow heart rate, low blood pressure, low body temperature, stunted growth, mental retardation, delayed sexual development.
Addison’s DiseaseDeficiency of mineralocorticoids/glucocorticoids, impairing stress response and electrolyte balance.
Cushing’s SyndromeExcess cortisol, causing high blood sugar, obesity, increased blood volume/pressure.
AldosteronismExcess aldosterone from adrenal cortical tumors, leading to high blood pressure and sodium retention.
Adrenal VirilismExcess androgens in females, causing male characteristics (e.g., beard, mustache).
GynaecomastiaEnlarged mammary glands in males due to excess estrogen.
Precocious PubertyEarly maturation of ovaries (before age 9 in girls) or testes (before age 10 in boys), producing ova/sperms (sexual pseudoprecocity).
EunuchoidismFailure of testosterone secretion in males, leading to underdeveloped/non-functional secondary sex organs, lack of accessory sex characteristics, no sperm production.
- Genetics - Definition: - Branch of biology studying inheritance and variation of characteristics from parents to offspring. - Term coined by W. Bateson (1905). - Inheritance: Process of passing traits via genes (core chromosome components). - Variation: Degree of difference between progeny and parents. - Mendel’s Experiments: - Gregor Johann Mendel (1822–1884), “Father of Genetics,” conducted experiments (1856–1863) on garden pea (Pisum sativum). - Chose peas for 14 contrasting traits across 7 characters, using pure homozygous plants (consistent for ≥3 generations). - Observations: - Crossed tall (TT) and dwarf (tt) plants; F1 generation all tall (Tt, hybrids). - F1 self-pollination produced F2 with tall and dwarf plants, showing both traits inherited but only tallness expressed in F1. - Introduced terms: - Dominant (expressed, e.g., T for tallness) and Recessive (masked, e.g., t for dwarfness). - Deduced two factors (alleles) per trait in reproductive cells; homozygous (TT, tt) or heterozygous (Tt). - Contrasting Traits in Pea Plants: -
CharacteristicDominantRecessive
Length of StemTallDwarf
Shape of SeedsRoundWrinkled
Colour of SeedsYellowGreen
Shape of PodsInflatedConstricted
Colour of PodsGreenYellow
Position of FlowersAxialTerminal
Colour of FlowersRedWhite
- Mendel’s Laws of Inheritance: - Derived from monohybrid and dihybrid crosses via crossing and pollination. - Laws: 1. Law of Dominance: - When two alternative alleles are present, one (dominant) expresses in F1, while the other (recessive) is masked; e.g., tallness (T) dominant over dwarfness (t). 2. Law of Segregation (Purity of Gametes): - Alleles don’t blend; both traits recover in F2, though recessive is hidden in F1. Each gamete carries one allele. - Example Monohybrid Cross: - Parents: TT (tall) × tt (dwarf) → F1: Tt (all tall). - F1 selfing → F2: TT, Tt, Tt, tt (3 tall: 1 dwarf, phenotypic ratio). - Proves dominance (T expressed in Tt) and segregation (Tt gametes carry T or t). 3. Law of Independent Assortment: - Genes for different traits segregate independently during gamete formation. - Example Dihybrid Cross: - Parents: YYRR (yellow round) × yyrr (green wrinkled) → F1: YyRr (all yellow round). - F1 selfing → F2: 9 yellow round, 3 green round, 3 yellow wrinkled, 1 green wrinkled (9:3:3:1 phenotypic ratio; genotypic 1:2:2:4:1:2:1:2:1). - Shows independent segregation of seed color and shape genes. - Key Terms: - Gene: - Heredity unit, coined by W. Johannsen (1909); Mendel called it “factor.” - Phenotype: - Morphological appearance of an individual. - Genotype: - Genetic constitution of an individual. - Alleles: - Alternate forms of a gene at the same locus on homologous chromosomes. - Back Cross: - F1 hybrid crossed with either parent. - Test Cross: - F1 hybrid crossed with recessive parent. - Exceptions to Mendelism: 1. Incomplete Dominance: - F1 hybrid phenotype is intermediate between parents, not resembling either; e.g., snapdragon, four o’clock plant (red × white → pink flowers due to partial red dominance). 2. Codominance: - Both alleles express independently in heterozygotes; e.g., ABO blood groups (IAIB → AB blood type, both A and B antigens expressed). 3. Multiple Allelism: - More than two alleles for a gene in a population; e.g., ABO blood groups (IA, IB, i alleles). - ABO Genotypes: -
Allele from Parent 1Allele from Parent 2GenotypeBlood Type

- Chromosomal Theory of Inheritance: - Rediscovered by de Vries, Correns, von Tschermak (1900); confirmed by Sutton and Boveri. - Chromosomes and genes occur in pairs; alleles on homologous chromosome sites. - Chromosome separation during meiosis explains allele segregation (Mendel’s laws). - Verified experimentally by Thomas Hunt Morgan. - Linkage and Recombination: - Linkage: - Genes on the same chromosome tend to stay together (tightly linked → low recombination; loosely linked → high recombination). - Recombination: - Generation of non-parental gene combinations during meiosis; frequency increases with gene distance on chromosome. - Sex Determination in Humans: - XX-XY system: - 23 chromosome pairs (22 autosomes, 1 sex chromosome pair). - Males: - XY (1 X, 1 Y); Females: XX (2 X, no Y). - Sperm determines offspring sex: - X sperm → XX (female); no-X sperm → XY (male). - Discovered by Henking (1891) as “X body” in insects; XO system in some insects (X sperm → female, no-X sperm → male). - Mutation: - Alteration in DNA sequence, changing genotype/phenotype; caused by mutagens (physical/chemical). - Types: 1. Gene Mutation: - Point Mutation: - Single base pair change; e.g., sickle-cell anemia. - Frameshift Mutation: - Base pair insertion/deletion, shifting reading frame. 2. Chromosomal Mutation: - Structural: - Loss/gain of DNA segment; e.g., cancer cell alterations. - Numerical: - Aneuploidy: - Gain/loss of chromosomes due to failed chromatid segregation. - Polyploidy: - Extra chromosome sets due to failed cytokinesis, common in plants. - Genetic Disorders: - Caused by absence, excess, or abnormal chromosome/gene arrangements. -

- Heredity and Evolution - Heredity: - Transmission of traits from parents to offspring via DNA, the molecule encoding genetic information. - DNA is the genetic material in most organisms; some viruses use RNA. - Packaging of Hereditary Material: - Eukaryotes: - Organized nucleus with nuclear envelope, nucleoplasm, chromatin, nucleolus. - DNA associated with histones, forming chromosomes. - Prokaryotes: - Nucleoid (unorganized, no nuclear membrane); circular DNA with proteins (not histones) binding to phosphate groups. - DNA (Deoxyribonucleic Acid): - Long polymer of deoxyribonucleotides; length defined by nucleotide number. - Nucleotide: - Nitrogenous base, pentose sugar (deoxyribose), phosphate group. - Nitrogenous Bases: - Purines (adenine, guanine), pyrimidines (cytosine, thymine in DNA; uracil in RNA). - Nucleoside: - Base + sugar via N-glycosidic linkage (e.g., deoxyadenosine). - Nucleotide: - Nucleoside + phosphate via phosphodiester linkage. - Structure: - Double helix, antiparallel strands (5′–3′ and 3′–5′), complementary base pairing (A–T, C–G). - Exons: - Coding DNA segments; Introns: Non-coding, removed in mature RNA. - Double helix model by Watson and Crick (1953), based on X-ray diffraction by Wilkins and Franklin. - Forms: - A-DNA: - Right-handed, 11 residues/turn. - B-DNA: - Right-handed, 10 residues/turn (Watson-Crick model). - Z-DNA: - Left-handed, 12 residues/turn. - Single-stranded in some viruses (e.g., φX174). - RNA (Ribonucleic Acid): - Single-stranded, pentose sugar (ribose), phosphate group; uracil replaces thymine. - Types: - mRNA (messenger), rRNA (ribosomal), tRNA (transfer). - Functions: - Genetic material in some viruses, catalyst in biochemical reactions. - DNA evolved from RNA. - Central Dogma (Francis Crick): - Genetic information flow: - DNA → (Replication) → DNA → (Transcription) → mRNA → (Translation) → Protein. - Evolution: - Gradual changes from simple to complex life forms over millions of years. - Origin of Life: - Universe began ~15–20 billion years ago (Big Bang theory), forming subatomic particles (protons, neutrons, electrons), then hydrogen, helium, lithium atoms. - Life originated ~3,600 million years ago (cyanobacteria fossils). - Spontaneous generation disproved by Louis Pasteur. - Oparin-Haldane-Miller-Fox Theory: Life arose via physicochemical processes forming inorganic/organic compounds. - Biological Evolution: - Genetic-level changes in allele frequency across generations, dependent on reproduction time. - Theories of Organic Evolution: - Lamarckism (Jean Baptiste de Lamarck, 1809): - Theory of Use and Disuse: - Frequently used organs strengthen, unused degenerate. - Inheritance of Acquired Characters: - Acquired traits passed to offspring (e.g., giraffe neck elongation); now discredited. - Darwinism (Charles Darwin, 1859, “On the Origin of Species”): - Natural Selection: - Nature selects fittest organisms based on inherited traits. - Observations from Galapagos finches (Darwin’s finches), showing adaptations. - Key Points: - New forms linked to life cycle/span. - Fitness based on inherited, genetic traits. - Organisms adapt to hostile environments. - Fitness results from adaptation and selection. - Mutation Theory (Hugo de Vries, 1901): - Evolution via sudden mutations in evening primrose (Oenothera lamarckiana). - Features: - Mutations create new varieties/species. - Mutations are discontinuous, inheritable, may occur in multiple individuals. - Agents of Evolution: - Mutation: - Random, slow changes in DNA, leading to new phenotypes/speciation. - Gene Flow: - Allele movement between populations. - Genetic Drift: - Random allele frequency changes in small, isolated populations. - These disrupt Hardy-Weinberg equilibrium, causing genetic variation and speciation. - Evidences of Organic Evolution: - Morphology and Comparative Anatomy: - Homologous Organs: - Same embryonic origin, different functions (e.g., frog, bird, human forelimbs). - Analogous Organs: - Similar functions, different origins (e.g., insect and bird wings). - Vestigial Organs: - Non-functional, ancestral remnants (e.g., snake limbs, human appendix); atavism: reappearance of ancestral traits. - Connecting Links: - Organisms with traits of two groups: -
OrganismConnecting Link Between

- Genetics: - Hybrids (e.g., mule from horse-donkey) resemble close relatives. - Embryology: - Biogenetic Law (Haeckel): Ontogeny recapitulates phylogeny; embryos resemble ancestral stages. - Fossils: - Remains (bones, teeth, shells) in sedimentary rocks; age determined by lead-uranium, carbon dating, fission track, electron spin resonance. - Coprolites: - Fossilized feces with phosphate salts. - Paleontology: - Study of life history via fossils. - Origin and Evolution of Man: - Human evolution: Developmental changes leading to modern humans. - Evolutionary Series: -

- Everyday Science: - First mammals: - Shrew-like. - Dinosaurs: - Land reptiles, e.g., Tyrannosaurus rex (~20 ft tall). - Mammals: - Viviparous, protect unborn young. - Lobefins: - Evolved into amphibians (ancestors of frogs, salamanders). - Future humans (Homo sapiens futuris, per H.L. Shapiro) may evolve via mutation, recombination, natural selection. - Health and Diseases - Health: - State of physical, mental, social, and emotional well-being; functional/metabolic efficiency. - Disrupted by infections, lifestyle issues (e.g., poor diet, lack of exercise), genetic disorders. - Maintenance: Balanced diet, hygiene, exercise, disease awareness, vaccinations. - Disease: - Abnormal body state with disturbed functions, identified by symptoms. - Types: 1. Congenital Diseases: - Present at birth; caused by genetic/hereditary errors, maternal malnutrition. - Neonatal: - Develop within first month; anomalies if detected in fetus. - Examples: - Cleft lip/palate, congenital heart defects, cystic fibrosis, neural tube defects. 2. Acquired Diseases (Post-fetal): - Develop after birth; divided into: - Communicable (Infectious) Diseases: - Transmissible via pathogens (viruses, bacteria, fungi, protozoans, helminths). - Viral Diseases: -

- Non-Communicable Diseases: - Not transmissible; caused by exogenous (nutritional deficiencies) or endogenous (hormonal) factors. - Types: A. Degenerative Diseases: -
DiseaseDescription
CancerUncontrolled cell growth (neoplasm); oncogenes involved. Benign (confined) or malignant (metastatic) tumors. Caused by physical (X-rays, UV), chemical (nicotine, caffeine), biological (viruses) agents. Symptoms: Unhealing wounds, lumps, bleeding. Types: Carcinomas (skin, glands), sarcomas (muscles, bones), gliomas (brain), melanoma (skin moles), lymphomas (lymphatic). Detection: Radiography, biopsy, MRI. Treatment: Chemotherapy, surgery, radiotherapy, immunotherapy.
Heart AttackMyocardial infarction; coronary artery blockage by plaque (fat, calcium). Symptoms: Chest pain, death if untreated. Causes: Shock, poor circulation.
DiabetesHigh blood glucose. Type 1: Insufficient insulin (children/adults, ketoacidosis risk). Type 2: Insulin resistance (common, adults). Symptoms: Weight loss, thirst, fatigue.
ArthritisJoint inflammation. Types: Rheumatoid (autoimmune), osteoarthritis (cartilage degeneration), gout (uric acid buildup). Symptoms: Pain, stiffness, swelling. Treatment: Therapy, surgery, medication.

C. Genetic Diseases: - Caused by chromosome/gene abnormalities (e.g., Progeria, Cri-du-chat, Patau’s syndrome). D. Mental Disorders: -
DisorderDescription
Alzheimer’s DiseaseProgressive dementia, common in >65, no cure, worsens to death.
DyslexiaReading difficulty, hereditary, affects language brain areas. Symptoms: Late talking, slow word learning.
SchizophreniaAltered reality perception, severe in men, late teens/early adulthood. Managed with medication, therapy.
- Immunity: - Ability to fight disease-causing agents; two types: A. Innate Immunity: - Non-specific, present at birth; includes barriers (skin, stomach acid, tears, WBCs). B. Acquired Immunity: - Pathogen-specific, developed post-birth via infection or vaccination. - Characteristics: - Specificity (distinguishes pathogens), Memory (intense response on re-exposure). - Types: - Active: - Body produces antibodies post-antigen exposure (slow, long-lasting, no side effects). - Passive: - Preformed antibodies transferred (e.g., maternal antibodies via placenta, colostrum IgA). - Antibodies: - Proteins from B-lymphocytes in response to antigens; T-cells aid production. - Types: -
ClassDescription
IgGMain circulating antibody, attacks microbes/toxins
IgAMain in secretions (saliva, milk), attacks microbes/toxins
IgETriggers allergic reactions
IgMLargest, circulating, 5 subunits
IgDMembrane-bound immunoglobulin
- Monoclonal Antibodies: - Identical antibodies from cloned immune cells, bind same epitope. - Concept by Paul Ehrlich (1908 Nobel Prize with Metchnikoff); used for syphilis treatment (1910). - Immune Responses: - Primary: - Low-intensity response to first pathogen exposure; stores memory. - Secondary (Anamnestic): - Intense response to re-exposure, highly specific. - Mediated by B- and T-lymphocytes. - Immunisation: - Strengthens immune system (T-cells, B-cells, antibodies) via antigen exposure. - Active: - Body produces antibodies; Passive: Antibodies introduced externally. - Allergies: - Hypersensitive immune response to allergens (e.g., dust, pollen); IgE-mediated. - Symptoms: - Sneezing, watery eyes, rashes, asthma. - Treatment: - Antihistamines, adrenaline, steroids. - Autoimmunity: - Immune system attacks self-cells; e.g., rheumatoid arthritis, Addison’s disease, Hashimoto’s. - Biomedical Techniques: - Apply biology/physiology to clinical medicine for diagnosis/treatment. - Types: 1. Invasive Techniques: - Involve skin penetration/body entry (e.g., surgery, catheterisation). - Examples: - Open Surgery: - Kidney/gallbladder removal, cardiac/neurosurgery. - Minimally Invasive: - Cryosurgery, microsurgery, angioplasty, laparoscopic surgery. - Angioplasty: - Widens arteries using balloon catheter/stent. - Organ Transplantation: - Replaces damaged organs (autografts: same body; allografts: same species). 2. Non-Invasive Techniques: - No body invasion; reduce risks vs. exploratory surgery. - Examples: - X-ray Radiography (Roentgen, 1895): - Uses X-rays for heart, lung, bone imaging. - Angiography: - Visualizes blood vessels using radio-opaque agents (e.g., iodine). - CT Scan: - Combines X-rays and computers for 2D/3D organ images. - MRI: - Uses magnetic fields/radio waves to image brain, spinal cord, joints, tumors. - Ultrasound (Sonography): - High-frequency sound (1–15 MHz) for imaging kidney stones, pregnancy, heart function. - Introduction to Biotechnology - Definition: - Term coined by Károly Ereky (1919); applied biology fused with technology. - Exploitation of biological agents (microorganisms, plants, animals) for human welfare via products/services. - Definitions: - US National Science Foundation: - Controlled use of biological agents (e.g., microorganisms, cellular components). - European Federation of Biotechnology: - Integrated use of biochemistry, microbiology, engineering for technological applications. - British Biotechnologist: - Application of biological organisms, systems, or processes. - Old Traditional Biotechnology: - Used since 5000 BC for wine, vinegar, curd, leavened bread via microorganisms. - Fermentation (Example): - Converts carbohydrates (sugar, starch) to acids, gases, alcohol using yeast/bacteria. - Examples: - Milk to Curd: - Lactobacillus converts lactose to lactic acid at 30–40°C. - Alcoholic Beverages: - Ethanol produced by yeast under anaerobic/low-oxygen conditions. - Modern Biotechnology: - Enhances natural microbial capabilities or discovers new ones. - Products: - Bt cotton, Bt brinjal, Flavr Savr tomato, humulin (recombinant human insulin from E. coli). - Principles of Biotechnology: 1. Genetic Engineering: - Alters DNA/RNA to change host organism phenotype. 2. Chemical Engineering: - Maintains sterile conditions for large-scale production of biotech products (e.g., antibiotics, vaccines, enzymes). - Genetic Engineering: - Manipulates genome to improve traits using recombinant DNA technology (joining DNA from different organisms). - Tools: - Vectors: - DNA molecules carrying foreign DNA, replicating in host cells. - Types: - Plasmids, bacteriophages (e.g., lambda, M13), cosmids, BAC, YAC. - Plasmids: - Circular, extrachromosomal DNA in bacteria/yeast; discovered by Hayes and Lederberg (1952). - Bacteriophages: - Viruses infecting bacteria, with DNA/RNA and protein capsid. - Vector Features: - Replicate in host, produce multiple recombinant DNA copies, small size (<10 kb). - Enzymes: -
TypeFunctionExamples
LysingBreaks cellular components to extract DNALysozyme (bacterial cells)
JoiningSeals DNA gapsLigase
CleavingBreaks DNA into fragmentsExonucleases (5′/3′ ends), Endonucleases (any nucleotide)
SynthesizingSynthesizes DNAReverse Transcriptase (RNA template), DNA Polymerase (DNA template)
- Host Cell: - Organism for propagating recombinant DNA (e.g., bacteria for cloning). - Techniques: 1. Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR): - Developed by Kary Mullis (1983, Nobel Prize 1993). - Amplifies DNA sequences via: - Denaturation (94°C): - Separates double-stranded DNA. - Annealing (55°C): - Primers bind to template DNA. - Primer Extension (75°C): - Taq polymerase (from Thermus aquaticus) synthesizes DNA, using Mg²⁺ cofactor. - Repeated cycles produce multiple DNA copies. 2. RAPD (Random Amplification of Polymorphic DNA): - PCR-based, amplifies random DNA segments for phylogenetic analysis. 3. RFLP (Restriction Fragment Length Polymorphism): - Differentiates organisms via DNA cleavage patterns; used in genetic fingerprinting, paternity testing. 4. DNA Fingerprinting (Alec Jeffreys, 1984): - Identifies individuals via unique DNA sequences (0.1% variation, except monozygotic twins). - Uses Variable Number Tandem Repeats (VNTRs/minisatellites) as genetic markers. - Samples: - Blood, saliva, semen, hair, stored tissues. - Applications: - Crime investigation, paternity disputes, endangered species breeding. 5. Gene Therapy: - Treats genetic diseases by replacing defective genes with functional ones. - Types: - Somatic: - Modifies non-reproductive cells. - Germ Line: - Modifies sperm/egg genomes. 6. Cloning: - Produces genetically identical organisms via Somatic Cell Nuclear Transfer (SCNT). - First suggested by Hans Spemann (1938, father of modern embryology). - Example: - Dolly the sheep (1996–2003, Roslin Institute, Scotland), cloned from udder cell; died of respiratory infections. - Landmarks: -

- Human Genome Project (HGP): - 13-year project (1990–2003) by US Dept. of Energy and NIH. - Aimed to map and sequence all human genes (~20,000–25,000 identified). - Linked to bioinformatics; sequence published in Nature (2001, full sequence 2003). - Applications: - Genetic tests for diseases (e.g., breast cancer, liver diseases). - First genetically engineered product: Synthetic human insulin (Genentech, 1978) via E. coli. - Applications of Biotechnology: - Medicine: - Drug discovery, pharmacogenomics (genetic response to drugs), genetic testing. - Vaccines: - Hepatitis B (yeast-produced viral proteins), experimental AIDS vaccines. - Antibiotics: -
